\mathbb{E}[{h(\boldsymbol{\Theta})}]\approx\sum_{n=1}^{N}h(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(n)}).

(2.5)

If it were generally easy to draw samples directly from
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, the Monte Carlo
story would end here. Unfortunately, this is usually intractable. We can consider the posterior form
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\frac{p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}
, where in many problems
p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
can be computed easily, but computing
p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is challenging due to integrals, summations, etc. In such cases, Markov chain Monte Carlo is especially useful.

2.3.1
Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)
algorithms, also called MCMC samplers, are numerical approximation algorithms.
Since MCMC algorithms directly sample the solution space, uncertainty estimates
are determined simultaneously with a “best” solution.
Additionally, provided that the data support them, multiple solutions are feasible.
Intuitively, it is a stochastic hill-climbing approach to inference, operating over the complete data set. This inference method is designed to spend most of the computational efforts to sample points from the high probability regions of true target posterior distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(
Andrieu et al. 2003
;
Bonawitz 2008
;
Hoff 2009
;
Geyer 2011
)
.

In this sampler, a Markov chain stochastic walk is taken through the state space
\Theta
such that the probability of being in a particular state
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
at any point in the walk is
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. Therefore, samples from the true posterior distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
can be approximated by recording the samples (states) visited by the stochastic walk and some other post-processing methods such as
thinning
. The stochastic walk is a Markov chain, i.e. the choice of state at time
t+1
depends only on its previous state—the state at time
t
. Formally, if
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
is the state of the chain at time
t
, then it follows that
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(2)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})=p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
. That is, Markov chains are
history-free
. This history-free property of Markov chains provides two main advantages:

Due to this history-free property, the Markov chain Monte Carlo methods can be run for an unlimited number of iterations without consuming additional memory space.

The history-free property also indicates that the MCMC stochastic walk can be completely characterized by
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
, known as the
transition kernel
.

We then focus on the discussion of the transition kernel. The transition kernel
\bm{K}
can also be expressed as a linear transform.
If
p_{t}=p_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is a row vector that encodes the probability of the walk being in state
\boldsymbol{\theta}
at time
t
, then
p_{t+1}=p_{t}\bm{K}
. If the stochastic walk starts from state
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
, then the distribution from this initial state is the delta distribution
p_{0}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, and the state distribution for the chain after step
t
is
p_{t}=p_{0}\bm{K}^{t}
. We can easily find that the key to Markov chain Monte Carlo lies in choosing a kernel
\bm{K}
such that
\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrm{lim}}p_{t}=p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, independent of the choice of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(0)}
. Kernels exhibiting this property are said to converge to an
equilibrium distribution

p_{eq}=p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}})
. Convergence is guaranteed if both of the following criteria are met (see, for example,
Bonawitz 2008
for more details):

p_{eq}
is an invariant (or stationary) distribution for
\bm{K}
. A distribution
p_{inv}
is considered an invariant distribution for
\bm{K}
if
p_{inv}=p_{inv}\bm{K}
;

\bm{K}
is
ergodic
. A kernel is called ergodic if it is
irreducible
(meaning that any state can be reached from any other state) and
aperiodic
(indicating that the stochastic walk never gets stuck in cycles).

There are a large number of MCMC algorithms, too extensive for comprehensive coverage in this context. Popular families include Gibbs sampling, Metropolis-Hastings (MH), slice sampling, Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, adaptive rejection sampling, and many others. Though the name is potentially misleading, Metropolis-within-Gibbs (MWG) was initially developed by
Metropolis et al. 1953
, and MH subsequently emerged as a generalization of MWG
(
Hastings 1970
)
. All MCMC algorithms are recognized as special instances of the MH algorithm. Regardless of the algorithm, the goal of Bayesian inference is to maximize the unnormalized joint posterior distribution and collect samples of the target distributions, which are marginal posterior distributions, later to be used for inference queries.

The most generalizable MCMC algorithm is the Metropolis-Hastings (MH) generalization
(
Metropolis et al. 1953
;
Hastings 1970
)
of the MWG algorithm. The MH algorithm extended MWG to include asymmetric proposal distributions.
In this method, it converts an arbitrary
proposal kernel

q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
into a transition kernel with the desired invariant distribution
p_{eq}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
In order to generate a sample from a MH transition kernel, the process involves drawing a proposal
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
and subsequently evaluating the MH acceptance probability by

P[A(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})]=\min\left(1,\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})q(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star})}{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}\right),

(2.6)

with probability
P[A(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})]
the proposal is accepted and we set
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}
; otherwise the
proposal is rejected and we set
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
. That is,

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star},\text{ with probability }P[A(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})];\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},\text{ with probability }1-P[A(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})].\end{array}\right.

(2.7)

Intuitively, we may find that the
\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}
term tends to accept moves that lead to higher probability regions of the state space, while the
\frac{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star})}{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}
term tends to accept moves that are easy to undo.
Since in MH, we only evaluate
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
as a part of the ratio
\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}
, we do not need to compute the intractable normalization constant
p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
as mentioned in Section
.

The key in MH is the proposal kernel
q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
. However, the transition kernel is not
q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
. Informally, the kernel
K(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
in MH is

p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\mathrm{accept})P[\mathrm{accept}]+p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\mathrm{reject})P[\mathrm{reject}].

While
Tierney 1998
introduced that the precise transition kernel is

\displaystyle K(

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)})=p(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})

(2.8)

\displaystyle=q(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})A(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})+\delta_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)})\int_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}}q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})(1-A(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\star}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})).

2.3.2
MC V.S. MCMC

As shown in previous sections, the purpose of Monte Carlo or Markov chain Monte Carlo approximation is to obtain a sequence of parameter values
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(2)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(N)}\}
such that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}h(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(n)})\approx\int_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}h(\boldsymbol{\theta})p(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta},

(2.9)

for any functions
h
of interest (in the case of continuous random variables). In other words, we want the empirical average of
\{h(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)}),h(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(2)}),\ldots,h(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(N)})\}
to approximate the expected value of
h(\boldsymbol{\theta})
under a target probability distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. In order for this to be a good approximation for a wide range of functions
h
, we require the empirical distribution of the simulated sequence
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(2)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(N)}\}
to look like the target distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. MC and MCMC are two ways of generating such a sequence. MC simulation, in which we generate independent samples from the target distribution, is in some sense the “true situation.” Independent MC samples automatically create a sequence that is representative of
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, which means the probability that
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(n)}\in{\mathbb{A}},\forall\,\,n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
for any set
{\mathbb{A}}
is

\text{Pr}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(n)}\in{\mathbb{A}})=\int_{{\mathbb{A}}}p(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta},

(2.10)

However, this is not true for MCMC samples, in which case all we are sure of is that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\text{Pr}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(n)}\in{\mathbb{A}})=\int_{\mathbb{A}}p(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}.

(2.11)

2.3.3
Gibbs Sampler

Gibbs sampling was introduced by Turchin
(
Turchin 1971
)
and later by brothers Geman and Geman
(
Geman and Geman 1984
)
in the context of image restoration. The Geman brothers named the algorithm after the physicist J. W. Gibbs, some eight decades after his death, in reference to an analogy between the sampling algorithm and statistical physics.

Gibbs sampling is applicable when the joint distribution is not explicitly known or is challenging to sample from directly.
Instead, it relies on the known and easily samplable conditional distribution of each variable.
A Gibbs sampler sequentially generates a draw from the distribution of each parameter or variable in turn, conditional on the current values of the other parameters or variables. Therefore, a Gibbs sampler is a componentwise algorithm. In our example, given some data
{\mathcal{X}}
and a probability distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
parameterized by
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{D}\}
. We can successively draw samples from the distribution by sampling from

\theta_{i}^{(t)}\sim p(\theta_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-i}^{(t-1)},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

(2.12)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-i}^{(t-1)}
is all current values of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
in the
(t-1)
-th iteration except for
\theta_{i}^{(t-1)}
. If we sample sufficiently long, these
\theta_{i}
values will be random samples from the distribution
p
.
If we sample new values in turn for each parameter
\theta_{i}
from Equation (
), we will eventually converge to draws from the posterior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
When doing this Gibbs sampler, we also have to discard the first
k
draws since it takes a while to converge (i.e.,
burn-in
).
And because the consecutive draws are correlated, we only use every
j
-th sample (i.e.,
thinning
).

In deriving a Gibbs sampler, it is often helpful to observe that

p(\theta_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-i},{\mathcal{X}})=\frac{p(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{D},{\mathcal{X}})}{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-i},{\mathcal{X}})}\propto p(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{D},{\mathcal{X}}).

(2.13)

That is, the conditional distribution is proportional to the joint distribution. We will get a lot of benefits from this simple observation by dropping constant terms from the joint distribution (relative to the parameters we are conditioned on).

Shortly, as a simplified example, given a joint probability distribution
p(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\mid{\mathcal{X}})
, a Gibbs sampler would draw
p(\theta_{1}\mid\theta_{2},{\mathcal{X}})
, then
p(\theta_{2}\mid\theta_{1},{\mathcal{X}})
iteratively.
The procedure generates a sequence of realization for random variables
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
:

(\theta_{1}^{(0)},\theta_{2}^{(0)}),\,\,\,(\theta_{1}^{(1)},\theta_{2}^{(1)}),\,\,\,(\theta_{1}^{(2)},\theta_{2}^{(2)}),\,\,\,\cdots,

which converges to the joint distribution
p(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
. Further details about Gibbs sampling can be found in
Turchin 1971
;
Geman and Geman 1984
;
Hoff 2009
;
Gelman et al. 2013
.

2.3.4
Adaptive Rejection Sampling (ARS)

The purpose of the adaptive rejection sampling algorithm is to provide a relatively efficient way to sample from a distribution from the large class of log-concave densities
(
Gilks and Wild 1992
;
Wild and Gilks 1993
)
. We provide a brief overview of the algorithm here, with additional details available in
Gilks and Wild 1992
and
Wild and Gilks 1993
.

Figure 2.4:
Rejection sampling. Figure is from Michael I. Jordan’s lecture notes.

Rejection Sampling

In rejection sampling, the objective is to sample from a target probability density function
p(x)
, given that we can sample from a probability density function
q(x)
easily.
Although the target density
p(x)
is unknown, the approach relies on establishing an envelop by considering
M\times q(x)
such that it covers
p(x)
for some
M>1
, as illustrated in Figure
.
This is expressed as:

\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}<M,\text{ for all $x$.}

(2.14)

Subsequently, when sampling
x_{i}
from
q(x)
, and if
y_{i}=u\times M\times q(x_{i})
lies below the region under
p(x)
for some
u\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(0,1)
, then we accept
x_{i}
; otherwise, it is rejected.

In essence, the method involves sampling
x_{i}
from a distribution and making an acceptance or rejection decision based on the comparison with the envelope.

Adaptive Rejection Sampling

Figure 2.5:
Adaptive rejection sampling. Figure is from Michael I. Jordan’s lecture notes.

The adaptive rejection sampling method, tailored for log-concave densities, is an extension of the rejection sampling.
The basic idea involves dynamically constructing an upper envelope (the upper bound on
p(x)
), serving as an adaptive replacement for
M\times q(x)
in rejection sampling.

As shown in Figure
, the logarithm of the density,
\log p(x)
, is considered. We then sample
x_{i}
from the upper envelope, and the sample is either accepted or rejected akin to rejection sampling.
In case of rejection, a tangent is drawn passing through
x=x_{i}
and
y=\log(p)
; and the tangent is used to reduce the upper envelope to decrease the number of rejected samples. The intersections of these tangent planes enable the formation of an envelope adaptively. To sample from the upper envelope, we need to transform from log space by exponentiating and using properties of the exponential distribution.

2.4
Bayesian Appetizers

This section delves into semi-conjugate priors with the Gibbs sampler and full conjugate priors without approximate inference, offering an in-depth exploration of Bayesian approaches.
Readers who already have a basic knowledge of Bayesian inference may choose to skip this section.

2.4.1
Beta-Bernoulli Model

We formally introduce a
Beta-Bernoulli
model to illustrate how the Bayesian approach works.
The Bernoulli distribution serves to model binary outcomes, i.e., outputting two possible values. The likelihood under this model corresponds to the probability mass function of the Bernoulli distribution with parameter
\theta
:

\mathrm{Bern}(x\mid\theta)=p(x\mid\theta)=\theta^{x}(1-\theta)^{1-x}\mathds{1}(x\in\{0,1\}).

That is,

\mathrm{Bern}(x\mid\theta)=p(x\mid\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1-\theta,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}x=0;\\
&\theta,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}x=1,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\theta
is the probability of outputting 1, and
1-\theta
is the probability of outputting 0.
The mean of the Bernoulli distribution is
\theta
.
Suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
are drawn i.i.d. from the Bernoulli distribution
\mathrm{Bern}(\theta)
. Then, the likelihood under the Bernoulli distribution with parameter
\theta
is given by

\displaystyle\text{likelihood}=p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\theta)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\sum x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{n-\sum x_{i}},

which is a distribution on
{\mathcal{X}}
and is called the
likelihood function
on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

And we will see the prior under this model is the probability density function of a
Beta distribution
:

\mathrm{prior}=\mathrm{Beta}(\theta\mid a,b)=p(\theta\mid a,b)=\frac{1}{B(a,b)}\theta^{a-1}(1-\theta)^{b-1}\mathds{1}(0\leq\theta\leq 1),

where
B(a,b)
denotes the
Euler’s beta function
and it can be simply regarded as a constant normalization term, and
\mathds{1}(a\leq x\leq b)
is a step function that has a value of 1 when
a\leq x\leq b
and 0 otherwise (when
x<a
or
a>b
). Figure
compares different parameters for the Beta distribution.
Specifically, when
a=b=1
, the Beta distribution reduces to a
uniform distribution
in the support of
[0,1]
.

Figure 2.6:
Beta probability density functions for different values of the parameters
a
and
b
.
When
a=b=1
, the beta distribution reduces to a
uniform distribution
in the support of
[0,1]
.
The mean, variance, and mode of the Beta distribution are
\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\frac{a}{a+b}
,

\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\frac{ab}{(a+b+1)(a+b)^{2}}
, and
\mathrm{mode}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\frac{a-1}{(a-1)+(b-1)}
if
a>1,b>1
, respectively.

A Beta prior is assigned to the parameter
\theta
of the Bernoulli distribution. The posterior is proportional to the product of the likelihood and prior densities, given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}=p(\theta\mid{\mathcal{X}})

\displaystyle\propto p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\theta)p(\theta\mid a,b)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\sum x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{n-\sum x_{i}}\times\frac{1}{B(a,b)}\theta^{a-1}(1-\theta)^{b-1}\cdot\mathds{1}(0\leq\theta\leq 1)

\displaystyle\propto\theta^{a+\sum x_{i}-1}(1-\theta)^{b+n-\sum x_{i}-1}\cdot\mathds{1}(0\leq\theta\leq 1)

\displaystyle\propto\mathrm{Beta}\left(\theta\,\bigg|\,a+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i},b+n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right).

We find that the posterior distribution shares the same form as the prior distribution; both stem from the Beta distribution, albeit with distinct parameters. When this happens, we call the prior a
conjugate prior
. A conjugate prior exhibits an advantageous structure, facilitating straightforward computations of the posterior probability density function, its derivatives, and the sampling process from the posterior.
The advantage of using conjugate priors lies in their ability to preserve the mathematical form of the prior throughout the updating process. This results in closed-form expressions for the posterior distribution, avoiding the need for complex numerical methods.

Remark 32

(Prior Information in Beta-Bernoulli Model)
.

Upon comparing the prior and posterior formulations, it becomes evident that the hyper-parameter
a
represents the prior count of occurrences of
1
’s in the output, while
b
corresponds to the prior count of 0’s in the output.
And the sum
a+b
aggregates the prior information about the sample size. Uninformative prior parameters can then be characterized by
a=b=1
, i.e., a uniform distribution.

Remark 33

(Bayesian Estimator)
.

From this Beta-Bernoulli model example, like the maximum likelihood estimator and the method of moment (MoM, i.e., using the moment information to calculate the model parameter), Bayesian modeling also serves as a type of point estimator. However, Bayesian models output a probability of the parameter of interest, denoted as
p(\theta\mid{\mathcal{X}})
in the example.

When predicting outcomes for new incoming data, instead of providing a direct model
p(x_{n+1}\mid\theta)
, the approach involves integration:

p(x_{n+1}\mid{\mathcal{X}})=\int p(x_{n+1}\mid\theta)p(\theta\mid{\mathcal{X}})d\theta.

In other words,
x_{n+1}
depends on
{\mathcal{X}}
. The observed data
{\mathcal{X}}
provide insight into
\theta
, which in turn provides information about
x_{n+1}
(i.e.,
{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\theta\rightarrow x_{n+1}
).

Example 34

(Amount of Data Matters)
.

Bayesian methods can be advantageous in cases of small sample sizes or sparse data, where traditional frequentist methods may encounter difficulties.
Suppose we have three observations for the success in the Bernoulli distribution:

1.

10 out of 10 are observed to be success (1’s);

2.

48 out of 50 are observed to be success (1’s);

3.

186 out of 200 are observed to be success (1’s).

So, what is the probability of success in the Bernoulli model? The normal answer to case 1, 2, and 3 are 100%, 96%, and 93%, respectively. But an observation of 10 inputs is rather a small amount of data, and the statistical significance may be limited due to potential noise.

To address this, we introduce a
Beta(1,1)
prior over the Bernoulli distribution. The posterior probability of success for each case would be
\frac{11}{12}=91.6\%
,
\frac{49}{52}=94.2\%
, and
\frac{187}{202}=92.6\%
, respectively. Notably, case 1 now exhibits a lower probability of success compared to case 2.

A Bayesian perspective on the problem naturally incorporates the amount of data as well as its average. This special case shown here is also called Laplace’s rate of succession
(
Ollivier 2015
)
. Laplace’s “add-one” rule (i.e., employing a
Beta(1,1)
prior) of succession modifies the observed frequencies in a sequence of successes and failures by adding one to the observed counts.
This modification improves prediction by avoiding zero probabilities and corresponds to a uniform Bayesian prior on the parameter.
Suppose further the prior parameters are
a=b=2
, Figure
compares the prior distribution and the posterior distributions for the three cases.

Figure 2.7:
The prior distribution is
\mathrm{Beta}(x\mid 2,2)
. The posterior distributions for the three cases in Example
are
\mathrm{Beta}(x\mid 12,2)
,
\mathrm{Beta}(x\mid 50,4)
, and
\mathrm{Beta}(x\mid 188,16)
, respectively.

The presented example underscores that Bayesian models incorporate prior information on model parameters, making them particularly useful for regularizing regression problems with limited data information. This characteristic has propelled the Bayesian approach to global recognition over the decades.

In this framework, the prior information
p(\theta)
and likelihood function
p(x\mid\theta)
collectively represent a rational person’s belief. And then the Bayes’ rule emerges as the optimal method for updating this person’s beliefs about parameter
\theta
given new information from the data
(
Fahrmeir et al. 2007
;
Hoff 2009
)
.

The prior information given by
p(\theta)
might be wrong if it does not accurately represent our prior beliefs.
Nevertheless, this does not mean that the posterior
p(\theta\mid x)
is not useful.
As encapsulated by the famous quote “all models are wrong, but some are useful”
(
Box and Draper 1987
)
. If the prior
p(\theta)
approximates our beliefs, then the posterior
p(\theta\mid x)
serves as a valuable approximation to posterior beliefs.

2.4.2
Bayesian Linear Model with Zero-Mean Prior

In the linear model, given the input data matrix
\bm{X}\in\real^{n\times p}
and the observation vector
\bm{y}\in\real^{n}
, the model considers the overdetermined system
\bm{y}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}
when
n>p
, where the vector
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\real^{p}
represents a vector of weights.
It often happens that
\bm{y}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}
has no solution since there are too many equations, i.e., the matrix
\bm{X}
has more rows than columns (
n>p
). Define the column space of
\bm{X}
by
\{\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}:\forall\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\real^{p}\}
and denoted by
\mathcal{C}(\bm{X})
. Thus the absence of a solution for the linear system
\bm{y}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}
implies that the observation vector
\bm{y}
is outside the column space of
\bm{X}
. Addressing this issue involves minimizing the mean squared error (MSE).

Instead of directly seeking the least MSE, we extend the analysis by introducing a Gaussian noise vector
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\in\real^{n}
such that
{\mathbf{y}}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I})
and
\sigma^{2}
is
fixed
(where
\mathcal{N}(\bm{a},\bm{B})
denotes a multivariate Gaussian distribution with mean
\bm{a}
and covariance
\bm{B}
. And a detailed analysis of this model can be found in
Rasmussen 2003
;
Hoff 2009
;
Lu 2022a
), this additive Gaussian noise assumption gives rise to the likelihood. Let
{\mathcal{X}}(\bm{x}_{1:n})=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}\}
be the observations of
n
data points, the likelihood function under this Gaussian additive noise model is

\mathrm{likelihood}=\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\bm{I}).

(2.15)

Suppose we specify a multivariate Gaussian prior with zero-mean and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
over the weight parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}
,

\mathrm{prior}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}).

Applying Bayes’ theorem, “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
,” we obtain the posterior

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}=p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2})\propto p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2})\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{n/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\}\times\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n/2}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\right\}\propto\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}),

where

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1},\qquad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\right).

Therefore, the posterior distribution is also a multivariate Gaussian distribution (same form as the prior distribution, i.e., a conjugate prior):

\mathrm{posterior}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}).

A word on the notation.

Note that we use
\{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}\}
to denote the posterior mean vector and posterior covariance matrix in the
zero-mean prior model
. Similarly, the posterior mean vector and posterior covariance matrix in
semi-conjugate prior
and
full conjugate prior
models will be denoted by
\{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}\}
, respectively, for clarity (refer to the sections below).

Connection to ordinary least squares (OLS).

In the Bayesian linear model, there is no general requirement for
\bm{X}
to have full rank.
Note further that if we assume full rank for
\bm{X}
(i.e.,
\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}
is invertible when
n>p
), in the limit, when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\rightarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}\bm{y}
, in which case, the
maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimator
from the Bayesian model reduces to the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimator. And the posterior is
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1})
, which shares a similar form with the OLS estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1})
under the Gaussian disturbance (see
Lu 2022a
).

Remark 35

(Ridge Regression)
.

In the least squares approximation problem, we use
\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}
to approximate
\bm{y}
.
However, two issues may arise: the model can potentially overfit and
\bm{X}
might not have full rank.
To address these concerns, in a ridge regression model, we regularize large values of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, prompting simpler models. Instead of minimizing
\left\lVert\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right\rVert^{2}
, the objective function becomes
\left\lVert\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right\rVert^{2}+\lambda\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\beta}\right\rVert^{2}
, where
\lambda
is a hyper-parameter that can be tuned accordingly, e.g., via cross-validation (CV):

\mathop{\arg\min}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})+\lambda\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

By differentiating and setting the gradient to zero, we obtain

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ridge}=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\lambda\bm{I}\right)^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y},

in which case,
(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\lambda\bm{I})
is invertible even when
\bm{X}
does not have full rank. Further details on ridge regression will be left to the readers.

Connection to ridge regression.

We realize that when we set
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{I}
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}\right)^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\bm{I}\right)^{-1}
. Since the posterior is
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1})
, the MAP estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
becomes
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}
, which shares the same form as the ridge regression by letting
\sigma^{2}=\lambda
.
This implies that the ridge regression estimator is a special instance of the Bayesian linear model with a zero-mean prior. And the ridge regression has a nice interpretation from the Bayesian approach—finding the mode of the posterior.

An illustrative example of this Bayesian linear model is presented in
Rasmussen 2003
, where the “well determined” (i.e., the distribution around the slope is more compact) slope of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is almost unchanged after the posterior process while the intercept, which is more dispersed, is shrunk towards zero. This is actually a regularization effect on the parameter as the ridge regression does.

2.4.3
Bayesian Linear Model with Semi-Conjugate Prior

We will employ the
Gamma distribution
as the prior density for the inverse variance (precision) parameter of a Gaussian distribution. The rigorous definition of the Gamma distribution can be found in Chapter
(Definition
) when we discuss conjugate models.
As for the reason of using the Gamma distribution as the prior for precision, we quote the description from
Kruschke 2014
:

Because of its role in conjugate priors for Gaussian likelihood functions, the Gamma distribution is routinely used as a prior for precision (i.e., inverse variance). But there is no logical necessity to do so, and modern MCMC methods permit more flexible specification of priors. Indeed, because precision is less intuitive than standard deviation, it can be more useful to give standard deviation a uniform prior that spans a wide range.

In the same context as Section
, we now consider the case where the variance
\sigma^{2}
of the Gaussian likelihood is
not fixed
. Again, the likelihood function is given by:

\mathrm{likelihood}=\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\bm{I}).

We specify a
non-zero-mean
Gaussian prior over the weight parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}
,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\mathrm{prior:\,}}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}({\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}{\displaystyle\gamma=1/\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{G}(a_{0},b_{0})},

where we differentiate from previous descriptions by blue text, and the function
\mathcal{G}(a,b)=\frac{b^{a}}{\Gamma(a)}x^{a-1}\exp(-bx)
is a Gamma distribution with parameters
a
and
b
. And the function
\Gamma(a)=\int_{0}^{\infty}a^{t-1}\exp(-a)dt
denotes the Gamma function.

Step 1, conditioned on
\sigma^{2}
.

Then, given
\sigma^{2}
, by Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
,” we get the conditional posterior density of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2})\propto p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2})\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{n/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n/2}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\right\}\propto\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}),

where the parameters are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\right).

Therefore, the conditional posterior follows from a Gaussian distribution:

\mathrm{posterior}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}).

Connection to the zero-mean prior model.

We highlight the connection between the zero-mean prior model and the semi-conjugate prior model as follows:

1.

We note that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}
in Section
is a special case of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
when
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
.

2.

And if we assume further
\bm{X}
has full rank, when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\rightarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}\bm{y}
, which reduces to the OLS estimate.

3.

When
\sigma^{2}\rightarrow\infty
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
is approximately approaching
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
, the prior expectation of parameter.
However, in the zero-mean prior model,
\sigma^{2}\rightarrow\infty
will make
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}
approach
\mathbf{0}
.

4.

Weighted average
: we reformulate
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{\sigma^{2}}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}

\displaystyle=(\bm{I}-\bm{A})\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\bm{A}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}
is the OLS estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and
\bm{A}=(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1})^{-1}\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{\sigma^{2}}
.
We observe that the posterior mean of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a weighted average of the prior mean and the OLS estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Thus, if we set the prior parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, the posterior mean of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
becomes precisely
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.

Step 2, conditioned on
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Given
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, again, by Bayes’ theorem, we obtain the conditional posterior density of
\gamma=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}

\displaystyle=p(\gamma=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})\propto p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\gamma)\cdot p(\gamma\mid a_{0},b_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{n/2}}{(2\pi)^{n/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\gamma}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\}\times\frac{{b_{0}}^{a_{0}}}{\Gamma(a_{0})}\gamma^{a_{0}-1}\exp(-b_{0}\gamma)

\displaystyle\propto\gamma^{(a_{0}+\frac{n}{2}-1)}\exp\left\{-\gamma\left[b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right]\right\},

and the conditional posterior follows from a Gamma distribution:

\mathrm{posterior\,\,of\,\,}\gamma\mathrm{\,\,given\,\,}\boldsymbol{\beta}=\gamma\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}\sim\mathcal{G}\left(a_{0}+\frac{n}{2},\left[b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right]\right).

Prior information on the noise/precision.

We can find an intuitive prior interpretation as follows:

1.

We notice that the prior mean and posterior mean of
\gamma
are
\mathbb{E}[\gamma]=\frac{a_{0}}{b_{0}}
and
\mathbb{E}[\gamma\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}]=\frac{a_{0}+\frac{n}{2}}{b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})}
, respectively. So the latent meaning of
2a_{0}
is the prior sample size for the noise
\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{\gamma}
.

2.

As we assume
{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}\bm{I})
, then
\frac{({\mathbf{y}}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}({\mathbf{y}}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\sigma^{2}}\sim\chi^{2}(n)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right]=\frac{n}{2}\sigma^{2}
. So the latent meaning of
\frac{b_{0}}{a_{0}}
is the prior variance of the noise.

3.

Some textbooks would write
\gamma\sim\mathcal{G}(n_{0}/2,n_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}/2)
to make this explicit (in which case,
n_{0}
is the prior sample size, and
\sigma_{0}^{2}
is the prior variance). However, this form of the prior may appear arbitrary or lack clear justification upon initial inspection.

Gibbs sampler.

By this Gibbs sampling method introduced in Section
, we can construct a Gibbs sampler for the Bayesian linear model with semi-conjugate prior:

0. Set initial values to
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
\gamma=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}
;

1. update
\boldsymbol{\beta}
:
\mathrm{posterior}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\gamma\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2})
;

2. update
\gamma
:
\mathrm{posterior}=\gamma\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}\sim\mathcal{G}\left(a_{0}+\frac{n}{2},[b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})]\right)
.

2.4.4
Bayesian Linear Model with Full Conjugate Prior

Introducing a Gamma prior over the inverse variance is equivalent to imposing an inverse-Gamma prior on the variance.
Same setting as the semi-conjugate prior distribution in Section
. We have the likelihood function:

\mathrm{likelihood}=\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\bm{I}).

But now we specify a joint Gaussian and inverse-Gamma prior over the weight and variance parameters by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\mathrm{prior:\,}}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\sigma^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}{\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{G}^{-1}(a_{0},b_{0})},

where again we differentiate this description from the previous ones with blue text.
Equivalently, we can formulate the prior into a joint one, which is called the
normal-inverse-Gamma (NIG)
distribution:

\displaystyle\mathrm{prior:\,}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\sim\mathcal{NIG}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0},a_{0},b_{0})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})\cdot\mathcal{G}^{-1}(a_{0},b_{0}).

Once again, applying Bayes’ theorem, “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
,” we obtain the posterior:

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X})\propto p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2})\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0},a_{0},b_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{n/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\top}(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{p/2}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{{b_{0}}^{a_{0}}}{\Gamma(a_{0})}\frac{1}{(\sigma^{2})^{a_{0}+1}}\exp(-\frac{b_{0}}{\sigma^{2}})

\displaystyle\propto\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{p/2}}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3})\right\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{(\sigma^{2})^{a_{0}+\frac{n}{2}+1}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left[b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3})\right]\right\},

where the parameters are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}).

Let
a_{n}=a_{0}+\frac{n}{2}+1
and
b_{n}=b_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3})
. The posterior is thus a NIG distribution:

\displaystyle\mathrm{posterior}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X}\sim\mathcal{NIG}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3},a_{n},b_{n}).

Connection to zero-mean prior and semi-conjugate prior models.

We highlight the connection of the full conjugate model to the zero-mean prior and semi-conjugate prior models as follows:

1.

If we assume further
\bm{X}
has full rank, when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}\bm{y}
, which reduces to the OLS estimate.

2.

When
b_{0}\rightarrow\infty
, then
\sigma^{2}\rightarrow\infty
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}
is approximately approaching
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
, the prior expectation of parameter. Compared to
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
in Section
,
\sigma^{2}\rightarrow\infty
will make
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
approach to
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
, where
\sigma^{2}
is a fixed hyper-parameter.

3.

Weighted average
: we reformulate
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\left(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}

\displaystyle=(\bm{I}-\bm{C})\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\bm{C}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}
is the OLS estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and
\bm{C}=(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1})^{-1}(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})
. We observe that the posterior mean of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a weighted average of the prior mean and the OLS estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Thus, if we set
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, the posterior mean of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
precisely equals
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.

4.

From the relationship of
a_{n}=a_{0}+\frac{n}{2}+1
, we know that
2a_{0}
is the prior sample size for
\sigma^{2}
.

5.

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3}^{-1}=\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}
: the posterior precision matrix (inverse covariance matrix) is equal to data precision
\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}
+ prior precision.

2.5
Variational Bayesian Inference

Monte Carlo approximate inference algorithms assess the quality of the approximation by using a sampling-based approach to approximate the target distribution, aiming to make the approximation as close to the target as possible.
These algorithms generate a large number of samples from a proposal distribution and use these samples to estimate properties of the target distribution, such as its mean, variance, or other moments. The quality of the approximation is thus directly related to the representativeness of the sample set and the efficiency of the sampling process.
In contrast, many other approximate inference techniques aim for this similarity by minimizing a specific metric that quantifies the difference between the approximation and the actual target distribution. One prominent method in this category is
variational inference (VI)
, also known as variational Bayesian inference. In VI, the chosen metric is the
Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
, which measures how one probability distribution diverges from a second, expected probability distribution. By framing the problem as an optimization task where the objective is to minimize the KL divergence, variational inference seeks to find the parameters of a simpler, tractable distribution (the variational distribution) that best matches the intractable posterior distribution. This approach allows for a trade-off between the complexity of the approximation and the computational resources required, often leading to efficient and scalable solutions for performing approximate inference in complex probabilistic models
(
Jordan et al. 1999
;
Wainwright et al. 2008
)
.

2.5.1
Motivating Model: Latent Variables and Alternating Method

Consider a statistical model that generates two random vectors
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, instead of one, according to a distribution from a parametric family
{\mathcal{F}}=\{f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=f(\cdot,\cdot\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}):\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta\}
, meaning
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\sim f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
for some
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\Theta
. However, we only observe realizations of the
{\mathbf{x}}
components, while the
{\mathbf{z}}
components remain unseen.
To be more precise, although the (unknown) parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
generates
N
pairs of samples
(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{z}_{1}),(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{z}_{2}),\ldots,(\bm{x}_{N},\bm{z}_{N})
, only
{\mathcal{X}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
are available to us. Therefore, the unobserved
{\mathbf{z}}
components are often called
latent variables
or
hidden variables
.
These variables play a crucial role in explaining the structure and variability in the observed data, even though they are not directly measurable.

In this context, the model’s parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is often considered a
global latent variable
, as it influences all observations and their associated latent variables. On the other hand, the individual hidden variables
\{\bm{z}_{i}\}
are known as
local latent variables
, since each one is paired with a single observation
\bm{x}_{i}
and is responsible for the variation in that particular data point (see the graphical representation in Figure
for a visual depiction of this relationship).

The hidden variable is not uncommon in real-life data sets. For example, in clustering, latent variables might represent the underlying groupings or clusters to which the observed data points belong
(
Beal 2003
;
Jain et al. 2017
;
Lu 2021c
)
. Other scenarios include topic modeling, where latent variables may indicate the topics that underlie a collection of documents, or in image analysis, where they could represent the features or structures that are not immediately apparent in the raw pixel data. The use of latent variable models (LVMs) allows us to uncover these hidden structures and make more informed inferences about the data-generating process.

The straightforward approach to solving such a model would involve finding the
maximum likelihood (ML) estimator
of the parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. This is done by maximizing the marginal likelihood, which can be expressed as:

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\,\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N})\equiv\prod_{i}\int p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i},

where we assume the support set of the random vector
{\mathbf{z}}
is continuous. When it is discrete, then
\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N})=\prod_{i}\sum_{\bm{z}_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}
.
However, in most cases, using the marginal likelihood function to perform the ML estimation is intractable; for example, when the support
{\mathbb{Z}}
is discrete, the sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}
contains
\left\lvert{\mathbb{Z}}\right\rvert^{N}
terms , making it challenging to both write out the expression completely and to optimize it as an objective function.

In the face of the intractability of directly computing the maximum likelihood estimate for models with latent variables, an effective strategy is to adopt an
alternating minimization/maximization approach
. This method involves an iterative process that alternates between two steps: estimating the posterior distribution over the hidden variables and then re-estimating the parameters of the model given this updated distribution.
To be more specific, assume the true parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
is provided ahead, then the hidden variables can be obtained by maximizing the posterior probability:

\text{(AM-Step 1): }\widehat{\bm{z}_{i}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\bm{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}.

Assume, in contrast, the latent variables are given ahead, then the maximum likelihood estimate of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is

\text{(AM-Step 2): }\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\text{MLE}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\,\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};\{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}).

This step refines the parameter estimates based on the full set of data, including the inferred latent variables.
This yields the alternating optimization approach for solving latent variable models; see Algorithm
.

The alternating optimization approach, as described, provides a systematic way to solve latent variable models.
However, it has certain drawbacks, particularly when the space of latent variables
{\mathbb{Z}}
is large.
At each iteration
t
, alternating maximization algorithm makes a “hard assignment,” assigning the data point
\bm{x}_{i}
to just one specific value of the latent variable
\bm{z}_{i}^{(t)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(we use the subscript
t
to denote the iteration count).
This approach can lead to the loss of valuable information, especially if there are other latent variable values
\bm{z}^{\prime}
such that
p(\bm{z}^{\prime}\mid\bm{y}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
is also significant, but still less than
p(\bm{z}_{i}^{(t)}\mid\bm{y}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
, causing these alternatives to be overlooked by the algorithm.

The
expectation-maximization (EM)
algorithm is designed to address this issue
(
Baum et al. 1970
;
Dempster et al. 1977
)
.
It addresses the issue by incorporating the uncertainty in the latent variable assignments. Instead of making hard assignments, the EM algorithm computes the expected value of the log-likelihood with respect to the current estimate of the posterior distribution of the latent variables. This soft assignment allows the algorithm to account for the contributions of all possible values of the latent variables, weighted by their posterior probabilities. As a result, the EM algorithm typically provides a more robust and accurate estimation of the model parameters compared to the simple alternating maximization approach.

Algorithm 1

Alternating Maximization for Latent Variable Models

1:

Observed data points
\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
;

2:

Initialize
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)}
;

3:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

4:

t=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

5:

while

t<C

do

6:

t=t+1
;

7:

for

i=1,2,\ldots,N

do

8:

Step-1:
\widehat{\bm{z}_{i}}^{(t)}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\bm{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
;

9:

end

for

10:

Step-2:
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\,\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};\{(\bm{x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{z}_{i}}^{(t)})\}_{i=1}^{N})
;

11:

end

while

12:

Output
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
;

2.5.2
ELBO and VFE.

Similar to the alternating maximization for latent variable models, the EM algorithm also aims to encourage an increase in the likelihood over multiple steps. As maximizing the marginal likelihood function directly is inefficient and intractable. The EM algorithm attempts to achieve this indirectly by finding a proxy function that lower bounds the marginal likelihood function and is easy to optimize.
If the proxy function is truly effective, maximizing it would also lead to an increase in likelihood. The key to the EM algorithm lies in introducing a proxy function called the
Q-function
that lower bounds the marginal likelihood function and turns the parameter estimation problem into a bivariate problem, with the two variables being the parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and the Q-function.

To delve into the EM algorithm, without loss of generalization, we assume the support of the hidden variable is continuous.
The logarithm of the
marginal likelihood
function is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\ln\int p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}=\sum_{i}\ln\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{explain data/expected energy}}\underbrace{-\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\mathrm{entropy}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathcal{F}}\left(q_{\bm{z}_{1}}(\bm{z}_{1}),q_{\bm{z}_{2}}(\bm{z}_{2}),\ldots,q_{\bm{z}_{N}}(\bm{z}_{N}),\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

where the inequality follows from the Jensen’s inequality since the logarithm function is concave.
This quantity
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
is called the
evidence lower-bound (ELBO, a.k.a., the marginal log-likelihood lower-bound)
.
As a hindsight (in the context of constrained EM optimization or variational inference), we want to maximize the ELBO when fixing
\boldsymbol{\theta}
constant.
The first term in the ELBO rewards variational distributions
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
to assign high probability to configurations of the latent variables that well-explain the observations (i.e.,
expected energy
); the second term encourages variational distributions that are entropic, i.e., that maximize uncertainty by spreading their mass on many configurations.

On the other hand, the ELBO can also be expressed as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}\left(\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}

(2.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln p(\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}+\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\ln p(\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})-\sum_{i}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left[q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\parallel p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]

\displaystyle\equiv-{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{VFE}}\left(\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

where
D_{\mathrm{KL}}[P\parallel Q]=\int P(x)\ln\left(\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\right)dx\geq 0
denotes the
Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
between
P
and
Q
and the equality is obtained only when
P=Q
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{VFE}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
is the
variational free-energy (VFE)
, a terminology from statistics physics
(
Neal and Hinton 1998
)
. The VFE is the negative entropy of
q_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z})
minus the expected energy under
q_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z})
:

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{VFE}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{negative entropy}}-\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{expected energy}}.

The above derivation also shows that maximizing the ELBO is equivalent to minimizing the KL divergence between the variational distribution
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
and the exact hidden variable posterior
p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
When fitting
q
to
p
by minimizing the KL divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}[q\|p]
, it has the property of
mode-seeking
or
zero-forcing
, which means minimization forces
q
to be zero where
p
is zero and hence makes it concentrate on one of the modes (called
reverse/exclusive KL
) .
On the other hand, when fitting
q
to
p
by minimizing the KL divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}[p\|q]
, it has the property of
mass-covering
or
mean-seeking
, which means we must make sure that there is some mass under
q
wherever there is some mass under
p
(called
forward/inclusive KL
, see Problem
).

2.5.3
EM for unconstrained optimization

The EM algorithm has its full generality due to
Dempster et al. 1977
, but the idea for specific problems had been around for several years before that
(
Baum et al. 1970
)
.
Similar to the alternating maximization approach, the EM algorithm alternates between two steps: the
E-step
, where it infers the posterior distributions of the hidden variables given the current parameter settings, and the
M-step
, where it maximizes the marginal log-likelihood function
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
based on the statistics obtained during the E-step.
Using the ELBO derived in (
), and denoting the
t
-th iteration of
q_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z})
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}
by
q^{(t)}_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z})
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
, respectively, the updates of E/M steps are

\boxed{\begin{aligned} &\textbf{E-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})&\leftarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}}{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}(t)}}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\};\\
&\textbf{M-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}&\leftarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q^{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}(t+1)}}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).\end{aligned}}

Therefore, the EM can be understood where the E-step approximates the conditionals of local latent variables
\{\bm{z}_{i}\}
and the M-step computes a conditional of the global latent variable
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
The two steps are repeated as necessary until the sequence of
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\}
converges.
Indeed, under very general circumstances, convergence to a local maximum can be guaranteed
(
Gupta et al. 2011
;
Jain et al. 2017
)
.
If the log-likelihood function is suspected to have multiple local maxima, it is advisable to run the EM algorithm multiple times, each time with a different initial value of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)}
. The ML estimate of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is then considered to be the best among the set of local maxima obtained from these various runs of the EM algorithm.

E-Step.

To obtain the E-step, we differentiate
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)
w.r.t. to
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
, subject to the normalization constraints:
\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.
The associated Lagrangian function takes the form (see, for example,
Boyd et al. 2004
):

L\left(q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)={\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)+\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}\left(\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}-1\right).

Setting the functional derivative (a.k.a., variational derivative) of the Lagrangian function with respect to
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
to zero yields

\int\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}-1+\gamma_{i}=0\implies q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=\exp(\gamma_{i}-1)p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i.

(2.18)

Plugging in the expressions into the Lagrangian function
L\left(q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\lambda}\right)
and taking maximum obtains

\int\exp(\gamma_{i}-1)p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})d\bm{z}_{i}=0\implies\gamma_{i}=1-\ln\int p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})d\bm{z}_{i}.

Substituting
\gamma_{i}
into (
), we have

q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i.

Therefore, the E-step is just the hidden variable posterior when fixing
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
.
Replacing the expression of
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
into
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)
, we may find that the ELBO is a
tight
bound of the logarithm of the marginal likelihood function
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, i.e., the bound becomes an equality (see Problem
):

{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)={\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}).

M-Step.

After obtaining the quantities
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, when fixing
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
, we turn to consider general
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. Thus, the E-step in EM algorithms are considered to obtain an
Q-function
in the literature
(
Gupta et al. 2011
;
Jain et al. 2017
)
:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})={\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)=\sum_{i}\int p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}d\bm{z}_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{$Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})$}}-\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{entropy of $p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}$})}.

Since the denominator term (the entropy term) in the
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)
does not involve a function of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, the M-step reduces to optimizing over the Q-function:

\displaystyle\textbf{M-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\sum_{i}\int p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}).

The full skeleton for the EM algorithm is shown in Algorithm
.
Since the E-step is a closed-form expression, we can simply reduce the E/M steps into a single step constructing the Q-function and iteratively update the Q-function. The Q-function can be further compactly written as

Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\underbrace{\bm{z}\sim p(\cdot\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})}_{\text{conditional prob.}}}\underbrace{\left[\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]}_{\text{log-joint prob.}},

such that the expectation is taken over the conditional probability of hidden variables (from last iteration) for the log-joint probability for the observed and hidden variables.

Algorithm 2

Expectation-Maximization (EM) Algorithm

1:

Observed data points
\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
;

2:

Initialize
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(1)}
;

3:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

4:

t=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

5:

while

t<C

do

6:

t=t+1
;

7:

E-step:
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
;

8:

M-step:
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
;

9:

end

while

10:

Output
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}
;

The Q-function possesses all the properties we want in our proxy. Parameters that maximize the function
Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
truly increase the log marginal likelihood
{\mathcal{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
. Even more significantly, for several applications, it is possible to both construct and optimize the Q-function extremely efficiently, e.g., the Gaussian mixture model and mixed regression problems
(
Jain et al. 2017
)
.

On the other hand, for each datum
\bm{x}_{i}
, we can define the
point-wise Q-function
:

\displaystyle Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\underbrace{\int p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}}_{\text{$\bm{Q}_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})$}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\bm{Q}_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),

where, letting
\bm{w}_{\bm{z}_{i}}=p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
, we have

\bm{Q}_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})=\int\bm{w}_{\bm{z}_{i}}\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}.

Therefore, upon a moment of reflexion, the advantage of the Q-function is revealed that we assign
\bm{x}_{i}
to each possible value of
\bm{z}
with weight
\bm{w}_{\bm{z}_{i}}
instead of finding the just one value of
\widehat{\bm{z}_{i}}^{(t)}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\bm{z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
.
In the Step-1 of the alternating maximization algorithm (Algorithm
), the optimal value
\widehat{\bm{z}_{i}}
under
p(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
is used to generate the parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for next iterations (hard assignment). However, the EM algorithms consider the full distribution
p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
to obtain the parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}
(soft assignment).

Stochastic EM algorithm.

In large-scale settings, we may apply stochastic gradient descent/ascent (see, for example,
Lu 2022d
) by randomly selecting a sample
\bm{x}_{i}
and executing a gradient update over the point-wise Q-function at each iteration
t
:

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}+\eta_{t}\nabla Q_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),

where
\eta_{t}
is the step size, and
\nabla Q_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})
is a
descent direction
(taking a positive step in this direction, on average, leads to an increase in the function value).

Maximum a posteriori (MAP EM).

More generally, we can impose any prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and consider the EM algorithm as

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{MAP}}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\sum_{i}\ln\int p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}+\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

The E-step remains the same, and the corresponding M-step reduces to

\textbf{MAP M-Step:}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})+\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

We will look at a simple but prevalent example for the EM algorithm, the Gaussian mixture model (GMM) , which is applied in applications such as clustering, density estimation, and topic modeling tasks (finds the topics within the associated documents).

Example 36

(Gaussian Mixture Model)
.

We consider a Gaussian mixture model with two modes, with
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\pi_{i},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in\{0,1\}}
,

f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\pi_{0}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}+\pi_{1}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{1},

where
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\cdot\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}),i=\{0,1\}
are multivariate Gaussian densities (Section
), the mixture components, and
\pi_{0}+\pi_{1}=1
are the mixture coefficients.
For brevity, we impose
\pi_{0}=\pi_{1}
and let
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}=\bm{I}
. We use the EM algorithm to find the mean parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\}
.
For each observed data point
\bm{x}_{i},i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, we associate it with a hidden variable
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
that indicates its underlying density:
\mathcal{N}_{0}
or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Therefore, the joint density and posterior are

\displaystyle p(\bm{x}_{i},z_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{z_{i}}(\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}_{z_{i}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{z_{i}}),

\displaystyle p(z_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\bm{x}_{i},z_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{p(\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}=\frac{p(\bm{x}_{i},z_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})}{p(\bm{x}_{i},z_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})+p(\bm{x}_{i},1-z_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}.

Therefore, the Q-function can be constructed by

\displaystyle Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\bm{Q}_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})=\sum_{i}\underbrace{\int p(z_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(z_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})dz_{i}}_{\text{$\bm{Q}_{\bm{x}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})$}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left\{p(0\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(0,\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})+p(1\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\ln p(1,\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\}

The corresponding EM algorithm thus can be implemented.

2.5.4
EM for constrained optimization

In the unconstrained EM optimization, we obtain
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i
.
However, the real problem can be far frustrating such that the data are explained by multiple interacting hidden variables and the hidden variable posterior
p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i
is intractable
(
Williams and Hinton 1991
;
Ghahramani and Jordan 1995
;
Beal 2003
;
Turner and Sahani 2011
)
.
The variational Bayesian approach thus address this issue by constraining the hidden variable posterior to a particular tractable form. Hence we call it
the EM algorithm for constrained optimization
or
the variational EM algorithm
.

Suppose we constrain the hidden variable posterior in a parametric family
{\mathcal{Q}}=\{q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=q_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}):\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda\}
, where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is called the
variational parameter
. Generally, the variational parameter can contain the corresponding observed data point
\bm{x}_{i}
for each hidden variable
\bm{z}_{i}
:
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})
with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}=\{\bm{x}_{i},\overline{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}\}
such that only
\overline{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}
can be updated iteratively.
The E-steps for
t
-th iteration becomes, following Equation (
) such that maximizing over the ELBO is equivalent to minimizing over the KL divergence,

\displaystyle\textbf{E-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\leftarrow

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\sum_{i}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left[q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\parallel p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right].

That is, finding the
variational posterior

q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})
that is closest to the hidden variable posterior
p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta})
in terms of KL divergence.
However, in such cases, the ELBO may no longer be tight anymore since the variational distribution may not reside within the family of the true hidden variable posterior.
The M-step is simply obtained by replacing the hidden variable posterior with the variational posterior in the Q-function:

\displaystyle\textbf{M-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\sum_{i}\int q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})d\bm{z}_{i}

It would be still confusing how the optimization works over the KL divergence between the variational posterior and the hidden variable posterior since we assume the latter is intractable. If we assume
q_{\bm{z}}(\bm{z}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
has easily computable moments (e.g., a Gaussian with only first and second moments), then the optimization is possible: obtaining the first and second moments of
p(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
We will delve more into this optimization in the sequel.

Mean-Field Approximation of Hidden Variables

To be more specific, let
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}(\bm{x}_{1:N})=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
be the observations of
N
data points and
{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathcal{Z}}(\bm{z}_{1:N})=\{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}\}
be the collection of hidden variables, where each observed variable
\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{P}
and each hidden variable
\bm{z}_{i}\in\real^{Q}
.
The
mean-field approximation
considers the fully factorized hidden variables:

q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij}).

Then the ELBO becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\theta})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}=\sum_{i}\int\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})\ln\frac{p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})}d\bm{z}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta})-\sum_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})\ln q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})d\bm{z}_{i}.

To obtain the E-step under mean-field approximation, we differentiate the ELBO in
t
-th iteration
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)
w.r.t. to
q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})
, subject to the normalization constraints:
\int q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})d\bm{z}_{ij}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},j\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
.
The associated Lagrangian function takes the form:

L\left(q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij}),\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)={\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)+\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}\left(\int q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})dz_{ij}-1\right).

Setting the functional derivative of the Lagrangian function with respect to
q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})
to zero yields

\displaystyle\ln q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\int\left[\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}q_{z_{ik}}(z_{ik})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\right]d\bm{z}_{i/j}+\gamma_{ij}-1

(2.19)

\displaystyle\implies q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}}\exp\left\{\int\left[\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}q_{z_{ik}}(z_{ik})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\right]d\bm{z}_{i/j}\right\},

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}
is the normalization constant,
d_{\bm{z}_{i/j}}
denotes the element of integration for all elements in
\bm{z}_{i}
except
z_{ij}
,

\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}
denotes the product of all elements except the
j
