-th item.
More compactly, this can be equivalently denoted by

q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}}\exp\left\{\mathbb{E}_{q(-z_{ij})}\left[\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\right]\right\},

(2.20)

where the expectation can be taken over all
i^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},j^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
except
\{i^{\prime}=i,j^{\prime}=j\}
.
Therefore, the ELBO is maximized iteratively w.r.t. to each factor
q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})
while keeping the remaining factors
q_{z_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}(z_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})
constant, where
i^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},j^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
and
\{i^{\prime}\neq i,j^{\prime}\neq j\}
.

We will give a motivating example to show how this mean-field approximation works in the next section.
An extension over the mean-field variational inference approach is called the structured VI
(
Saul et al. 1996
)
, which incorporates dependencies among variables, resulting in a more accurate approximation of the posterior probability density. However, this comes with a trade-off, as the inclusion of these dependencies can complicate the variational optimization problem, making it harder to solve.

2.5.5
Variational Bayesian Inference

In the EM algorithm, we derive the ELBO with respect to the hidden variables. Similarly, the variational Bayesian (VB) inference or variational inference (VI) considers the ELBO with respect to both the hidden variables and the model parameters (note previously, we treat hidden variables and model parameters together with
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for the non-variational Bayesian inference).
As a matter of fact, the EM algorithm with constrained optimization can be viewed as a special type of VI that focuses solely on the hidden variables, without including the model parameters. This perspective highlights the connection between the EM algorithm and variational inference methods, emphasizing that the EM algorithm can be seen as a simplified form of VI where the optimization is restricted to the hidden variables alone.

As previously assumed, we denote the observed variables by
{\mathcal{X}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
, the hidden variables by
{\mathcal{Z}}=\{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}\}
.
Same as the latent variable model, we assume the underlying two random vectors
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
follow from a parametric family
{\mathcal{F}}=\{f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=f(\cdot,\cdot\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}):\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta\}
, meaning
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{z}})\sim f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
for some
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\Theta
.
The difference lies in, under the Bayesian framework, the prior probability distribution for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
with hyper-parameters
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, representing the plausibility of each possible value of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, since the parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
themselves are unknown, before observing the data (see graphical representation in Figure
):

p(\boldsymbol{\theta},{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})=p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

where
p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\prod_{i}^{N}p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
under the i.i.d. assumption.

Therefore, the marginal likelihood of a model,
p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, can be lower bounded using the ELBO with hidden variables and parameters:

\displaystyle\ln p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\ln\int p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}=\ln\int q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\frac{p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

\displaystyle\geq\int q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}},

where the inequality is again from the Jensen’s inequality.
Following the E-step of the EM algorithm, when fixing
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)}
for the
t
-th iteration, it leads to
q^{(t+1)}({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})
; this in turn, when substituted into the ELBO, attains the equality for the lower-bound.
However, the update does not simplify the problem since
p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})
can be intractable due to the normalizing constant.
Therefore, we further assume the hidden variable and the model parameter factorize:
q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Thus, the ELBO inequality becomes:

\displaystyle\ln p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq\int q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q({\mathcal{Z}},\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

\displaystyle=\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

\displaystyle=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left[\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})}d{\mathcal{Z}}+\ln\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}}),q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right),

where the last equality follows from the data are drawn i.i.d. from
f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
for some
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\Theta
.
And we assume factorized
q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})
with

q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
such that

{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigg[\underbrace{\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}}_{{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}+\ln\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\bigg]d\boldsymbol{\theta},

where we notice that the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\cdot)
for variational Bayesian inference contains the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot)
for latent variable models, except a dependence on the hyper-parameter
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
(Section
).

Teriminology.

We refer to the ELBO mentioned above as the
general factorized framework
. In the following sections, we will explore several alternative frameworks. Different problems may concern different frameworks.

Similar to the EM update, we can derive the variational Bayesian EM updates that iteratively maximize
{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
with respect to the factorized model parameters and hidden variables:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} &\textbf{VBE-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})&\leftarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}}{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q^{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}(t)}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\};\\
&\textbf{VBM-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})&\leftarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q^{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}(t+1)}}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right).\end{aligned}}

VBE-Step.

To obtain the E-step, we differentiate
{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)
w.r.t. to
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
, subject to the normalization constraints:
\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.
The associated Lagrangian function takes the form:

L\left(q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)={\mathcal{F}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)+\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}\left(\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}-1\right).

Taking the functional derivatives (a.k.a., variatioanl derivatives) of
L\left(q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\lambda}\right)
with respect to
q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
and equating to zero yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L\left(q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\lambda}\right)}{\partial q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left[\ln p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})+\gamma_{i}-1\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}=0

\displaystyle\implies\ln q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=\int q^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left[\ln p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}-\underbrace{\int q^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})(1-\gamma_{i})d\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\ln{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}},

where
{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}
is a normalization constant with respect to
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})
.
Therefore, the VBE-step is obtained by

\textbf{VBE-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}}\exp\left\{\int q^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left[\ln p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,i.\\

VBM-Step.

Similarly, we take functional derivatives of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}}),q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)
with respect to
q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
The associated Lagrangian function takes the form:

L\left(q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)={\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}}),q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)+\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}-1\right).

This results in

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L\left(q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)}{\partial q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}=\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})}d{\mathcal{Z}}+\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-1+\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=0

\displaystyle\implies\ln q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})\ln p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}|\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})d{\mathcal{Z}}+\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\underbrace{(1-\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})+\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})\ln q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})d{\mathcal{Z}}}_{\ln{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},

where
{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a normalization constant with respect to
q^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Therefore, the VBM-step is

\textbf{VBM-Step: }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\exp\left\{\int q_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}})\ln p({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})d{\mathcal{Z}}\right\}.

Alternative Formulations

We may notice that, when
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is treated as a point estimate rather than being drawn from a distribution
q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, then the problem reduces to the latent variable model discussed in the EM algorithm problem (Section
). In this case, the ELBO reduces to the form given in Equation (
).
The variational EM algorithm thus can be seen as a special type of variational inference.
There are also alternative formulations of the variational Bayesian inference problem, which can provide different perspectives and potentially more flexible or efficient ways to perform different inference problems.

Constrained framework.

In Section
, we considered the constrained framework for EM algorithm, where
\bm{z}_{i}\sim q(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}),i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.
Similarly, in the variational Bayesian inference problem described above, we can also constrain each
\bm{z}_{i}\sim q(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}),i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
. The corresponding graphical representation is shown in Figure
.
Optimization over ELBO then becomes the following.

Remark 37

(Constrained VI)
.

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

(2.21)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\ln q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}-\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}.

No hidden variables, max ELBO as min of KL divergence.

When we do not assume any hidden variables (local latent variables), the ELBO reduces to

Remark 38

(No Hidden Variables)
.

\displaystyle\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}

(2.22)

\displaystyle=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}d\boldsymbol{\theta}+\ln p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=-D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left[q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\|p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]+\ln p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

Therefore, as claimed in the very beginning of this sectioon, variational inference is the optimization of minimizing the distance between the variational distribution and the posterior distribution, measured by KL divergence.

A unified framework.

More generally, we can absorb the hidden variables into the model parameters
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\theta},{\mathcal{Z}}\}
.
The unified framework considers more general cases, e.g., the number of hidden variables may not be equal to the number of observed data points.
For instance, in the context of topic modeling, a document can be considered as a mixture of topics, where a distribution over words characterizes each topic. The words are observed variables, while the topics are the assumed hidden variables; apparently, their total number of points are not the same
(
Blei et al. 2003
;
Blei 2012
)
.
In this setting, each document is represented as a combination of multiple topics, and each topic is defined by a probability distribution over the vocabulary. The observed data (words) are generated from these underlying topic distributions, but the exact assignment of words to topics is not directly observable. The goal of the model is to infer the topic structure and the distribution of topics within each document, even though the number of words and the number of topics do not align one-to-one.
Then the ELBO becomes, just the same as (
) (the same form as the one has only the model parameters with no hidden variables):

Remark 39

(Unified VI)
.

\displaystyle\int q_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\boldsymbol{\omega})}d\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle=-D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left[q_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\|p(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]+\ln p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

(2.23)

Mean-Field Approximation of Hidden Variables and Model Parameters

To be more specific, let
{\mathcal{X}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
be the observations of
N
data points and
{\mathcal{Z}}=\{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}\}
be the collection of hidden variables, where each observed variable
\bm{x}_{i}\in\real^{P}
, each hidden variable
\bm{z}_{i}\in\real^{Q}
, and the model parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\real^{D}
.
The mean-field approximation considers the fully factorized model parameters and hidden variables:

q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\prod_{j=1}^{D}q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij}).

Then the ELBO becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

\displaystyle=\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\left[\sum_{i}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\ln\frac{p(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})}d\bm{z}_{i}+\ln\frac{p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{j=1}^{D}q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})\left[{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})+{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta},

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int\prod_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\sum_{j=1}^{Q}q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})\ln q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})d\bm{z}_{i},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\sum_{j=1}^{D}\ln q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j}).

Update for local latent variables.

The update for hidden variables under the mean-field approximation is just similar to the one in the EM algorithm. To maintain consistency and clarity, we will derive it here; the notable difference will be denoted in
blue
text.
To obtain the E-step under the mean-field approximation, we differentiate the ELBO in
t
-th iteration
{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\right)
w.r.t. to
q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})
, subject to the normalization constraints:
\int q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})d\bm{z}_{ij}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},j\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
.
The associated Lagrangian function takes the form:

L\left(q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij}),\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right)={\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)+\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}\left(\int q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})dz_{ij}-1\right).

Setting the functional derivative of the Lagrangian function with respect to
q_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})
to zero yields

\displaystyle\ln q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\int_{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\int_{\bm{z}}\left[\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}q_{z_{ik}}(z_{ik})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right]d\bm{z}_{i/j}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}d\boldsymbol{\theta}}+\gamma_{ij}-1

(2.24)

\displaystyle\implies q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}}\exp\left\{\int_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\int_{\bm{z}}\left[\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}q_{z_{ik}}(z_{ik})\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right]d\bm{z}_{i/j}d\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\},

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}
is the normalization constant,
d_{\bm{z}_{i/j}}
denotes the element of integration for all elements in
\bm{z}_{i}
except
z_{ij}
,

\prod_{k\neq j}^{Q}
denotes the product of all elements except the
j
-th item.
More compactly, this can be equivalently denoted by

q^{(t+1)}_{z_{ij}}(z_{ij})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{ij}}\exp\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\mathbb{E}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{q(-z_{ij})}\left[\ln p(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.2109,0.7422}\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right]\right]\right\},

(2.25)

where the expectation can be taken over all
i^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},j^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
except
\{i^{\prime}=i,j^{\prime}=j\}
.

Update for model parameters (global latent variables).

Similarly, we differentiate the ELBO in
t
-th iteration
{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)
w.r.t. to
q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, subject to the normalization constraints:
\int q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})d\theta_{j}=1
for all
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,D\}
. The associated Lagrangian function is

L(q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j}),\boldsymbol{\nu})={\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)+\sum_{j}\nu_{j}\left(\int q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})d\theta_{j}-1\right).

Setting the functional derivative of the Lagrangian function with respect to
q_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})
to zero yields

\displaystyle\ln q^{(t+1)}_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})=\int\prod_{k\neq j}^{D}q_{\theta_{k}}(\theta_{k})\left[{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{(t+1)},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\alpha})+{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-j}-1+\nu_{j}

\displaystyle\implies q^{(t+1)}_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}\exp\left\{\int\prod_{k\neq j}^{D}q_{\theta_{k}}(\theta_{k})\left[{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{z}}({\mathcal{Z}}^{(t+1)},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\alpha})+\ln p(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]d\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-j}\right\},

where
d{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-j}}
denotes the element of integration for elements in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
except
\theta_{j}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
is a normalization constant.
Notably, when there are
no hidden variables
(
or the unified framework in (
)
), the update for the model parameters reduces to

q^{(t+1)}_{\theta_{j}}(\theta_{j})=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}\exp\left\{\int\prod_{k\neq j}^{D}q_{\theta_{k}}(\theta_{k})\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})d\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-j}\right\}.

(2.26)

Figure 2.8:
Variational distribution and true posterior distribution of Bayesian linear models in two-dimensional case; projection onto a plane.

Example 40

(Bayesian Linear Regression)
.

We consider the Bayesian linear regression in Section
and the notations therein; while we fix
\sigma^{2}
constant.
The prior and likelihood are
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0})
and
\bm{y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{X}\boldsymbol{\beta},\sigma^{2}\bm{I})
, respectively.
Therefore, the model assumes no hidden variables and only model parameters
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
The posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2})
, where

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}

and

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\right)
.
Suppose model parameters admit the following partition, within two-dimensional space:

\boldsymbol{\beta}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}\\
\beta_{2}\end{bmatrix},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}\\
b_{2}\end{bmatrix},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\sigma_{11}&\sigma_{12}\\
\sigma_{21}&\sigma_{22}\end{bmatrix},\,\,\sigma_{12}=\sigma_{21}.

Then, under the mean-field approximation (
), we have

\displaystyle\ln q_{\beta_{1}}(\beta_{1})

\displaystyle=\int q_{\beta_{2}}(\beta_{2})\ln\underbrace{p(\bm{y},\bm{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0},\sigma^{2})}_{\propto p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\bm{y},\bm{X},\sigma^{2})}d\beta_{2}+{\mathcal{C}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int q_{\beta_{2}}(\beta_{2})\left\{-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\right\}d\beta_{2}+{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{1}^{2}\sigma_{11}+\beta_{1}b_{1}\sigma_{11}-\beta_{1}\sigma_{12}(\mathbb{E}_{q_{\beta_{2}}(\beta_{2})}[\beta_{2}]-b_{2})+{\mathcal{C}}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\ln\mathcal{N}(\beta_{1}\mid\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\sigma^{2}}),

where
\widehat{\mu}=b_{1}-\frac{\sigma_{12}}{\sigma_{11}}(\mathbb{E}_{q_{\beta_{2}}(\beta_{2})}[\beta_{2}]-b_{2})
and
\widehat{\sigma^{2}}=\sigma_{11}^{-1}
. The variational distribution of
\beta_{2}
can be computed similarly due to symmetry.
We note that the variational distribution follows a Gaussian distribution even though we have not assumed anything about the variational distribution.
This is not always the case; and we will provide an example that needs to assume the form of the variational distribution a priori in Section
.
A caricature of variational distribution is given in Figure
.
The variational parameter
\widehat{\sigma^{2}}=\sigma_{11}^{-1}
indicates that the variance of
q_{\beta_{1}}(\beta_{1})
is equal to the variance of the conditional distribution
p(\beta_{1}\mid\beta_{2},{\mathcal{X}})
, which is smaller than the variance of the marginal distribution
p(\beta_{1}\mid{\mathcal{X}})
, and therefore, mean-field VI under-estimates the posterior uncertainty in this case.

2.5.6
Monte Carlo/Stochastic Variational Inference

We should emphasize that the book primarily focuses on MCMC methods, specifically Gibbs sampling, for Bayesian matrix factorizations. We only briefly touch upon their variational inference alternatives.
However, we do provide a brief overview of an important topic within VI:
black-box VI (BBVI), also known as Monte Carlo VI (MCVI)

(
Wingate and Weber 2013
;
Ranganath et al. 2014
;
Li 2018
)
, which is of greater interest to practitioners, generalizes the VI framework derived above to more complex models (e.g., non-conjugate exponential families), and in which the variational object is obtained via Monte Carlo samples of gradient and stochastic optimization (see, for example,
Lu 2022d
).

For compactness, we use the
unified framework
discussed in (
) that treats the hidden variables and model parameters altogether
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\theta},{\mathcal{Z}}\}
.
Suppose the variational density is parameterized by
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, then the ELBO is

{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ln\frac{p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}d\boldsymbol{\omega}=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right].

(2.27)

Theorem 41

(Gradient of ELBO)
.

Consider the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
), its gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\big[\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\}\big].

(2.28)

Note the value
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is called the score function in statistics.

of Theorem
.

To see this, using product rule and exchange derivatives with integrals via the dominated convergence theorem , we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}-\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right)d\boldsymbol{\omega}.

Since
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})=[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})]q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, the first term becomes

\displaystyle\int\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}[\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})]q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\big[\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\}\big].

The second term, the expected value of the score function, simplifies to

\displaystyle\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right)d\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\left[\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\right]=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})d\boldsymbol{\omega}=\mathbf{0}.

This concludes the result.
∎

As long as we obtain the gradient of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, we can update the variational parameter
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
by
gradient ascent
:

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t)}+\eta_{t}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t)}),

where
\eta_{t}
is the step size, and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is a descent direction (again, taking a positive step in this direction leads to an increase in the function value).
Since the expectation over
\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
can be
intractable
, we can compute the noisy unbiased estimate of the gradient with Monte Carlo samples (Monte Carlo gradients), hence the name Monte Carlo VI:

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\},\,\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}).

(2.29)

If we further use stochastic mini-batch sampling, then the stochastic gradient becomes, called the stochastic MC gradient,

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\}\left\{\frac{N}{\left\lvert{\mathcal{K}}\right\rvert}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{K}}}\ln p(\bm{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right\},\,\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}|\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

where
{\mathcal{K}}
is a sampled set that contains
\left\lvert{\mathcal{K}}\right\rvert
samples from
{\mathcal{X}}
,
N
is the total number of observed points.
The general algorithm of MCVI is summarized in Algorithm
.

Algorithm 3

Monte Carlo Variational Inference (MCVI)

1:

Observed data points
{\mathcal{X}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
;

2:

Initialize
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(1)}
;

3:

Choose the maximal number of iterations
C
;

4:

t=0
;
\triangleright
Count for the number of iterations

5:

while

t<C

do

6:

t=t+1
;

7:

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t)}+\eta_{t}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t)})
;

8:

end

while

9:

Output
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(t)}
;

Reparameterization Trick

The MCVI can be made more tractable using the
reparameterization trick

(
Kingma and Max 2013
;
Ho et al. 2020
)
.
Given a distribution
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, if the sampling
\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}=f(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})
.
Then the expectation of any function
g(\boldsymbol{\theta})
can be equivalently expressed as

\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[g(f(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}))\right].

Thus, the computation benefits when it is hard to sample from
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
directly or the variance of the MC gradient is too large so as to slow down the convergence
(
Kingma and Max 2013
)
.

In the variational inference context, the reparameterization trick asks the following:

\boldsymbol{\omega}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\rightarrow\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}),\boldsymbol{\omega}=f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}),

where the variational distribution
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and the variable transformation function
\boldsymbol{\omega}=f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})
depend on the same parameter
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.

Theorem 42

(Gradient of ELBO under Reparameterization)
.

Consider the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
) and the reparameterization with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}),\boldsymbol{\omega}=f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})
, its gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[\nabla_{f}\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\nabla_{f}\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})].

(2.30)

of Theorem
.

The gradient of
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
becomes, using the chain rule and the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})]-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[\nabla_{f}\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\right]-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right].

The second term is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})|_{\boldsymbol{\omega}=f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}]+\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\nabla_{f}\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})]+\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}[\nabla_{f}\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})],

where the first term is
\mathbf{0}
from the proof of Theorem
.
This concludes the result.
∎

Therefore, the MC gradient of the ELBO under the reparameterization trick becomes

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\big\{\nabla_{f}\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})|\boldsymbol{\alpha})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})-\nabla_{f}\ln q(f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})|\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})\big\},\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}).

The MC approximation for stochastic mini-batch sampling can be derived similarly, and we shall not repeat here.

Example 43

(Reparameterization by Gaussian)
.

The most motivating example for this reparameterization trick is assuming Gaussian densities for variational distributions.
We encourage the reader to momentarily skip to §
to look at the properties of multivariate Gaussian distributions before returning to proceed with this example.
To be more specific, we assume
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
admits the Cholesky decomposition
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\bm{L}\bm{L}^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},\bm{L}\}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\omega}
can be equivalently sample from
f_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})=\boldsymbol{\mu}+\bm{L}\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{I})
.
Therefore, the MC gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\bm{L}
are

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\left\{\nabla_{f}\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f)|_{f=\boldsymbol{\mu}+\bm{L}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(-\bm{L}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})\right\},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{L}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\left\{\nabla_{f}\ln p({\mathcal{X}},f)|_{f=\boldsymbol{\mu}+\bm{L}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(-\bm{L}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{I}),

where we use the fact that

\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})=-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right\rvert-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\mu})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\mu})+{\mathcal{C}}
.

Variance Reduction for MCVI Gradients with Rao-Blackwellization

Although the stochastic MC gradient of
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is an unbiased estimator of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, the variance can be too large such that the gradient update may jump into wrong directions and the convergence can be too slow. Variance reduction is the key in stochastic gradient descent/ascent to ensure more stable and efficient optimization.

The original
Rao-Blackwell theorem
states that
(
Rao et al. 1973
;
Blackwell 1947
)
, if
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is an unbiased estimator of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, and
T
is a sufficient statistic for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, then
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\mid T]
is also an unbiased estimator of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, and

\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}]\leq\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}],

where the equality is attained if and only
\Pr[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}]=1
.
In the context of variational inference, we consider the parameters
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\}
, and we would like to estimate the expectation for any function
g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})
given the variational distribution
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})
:
\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]
.
Let
g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]
, then the variance can be decomposed, similar to the bias-variance decomposition , by

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})\right]

(2.31)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}\left[\left(g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})-\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}\left[\left(g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})-g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})+g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})-\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]+\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}\left[\left\{g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})-\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})],

where we use the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})]
.

Returning to the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
), we can decompose the parameter
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\}
and consider the factorized model:
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})=q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})
. With this factorization, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 44

(Gradient of ELBO with Rao-Blackwellization)
.

Consider the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
) with factorized variational distribution
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})=q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\}
, its gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
is

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})}\bigg[\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\big[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big]-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\bigg].

(2.32)

The gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
can be derived similarly due to symmetry.

of Theorem
.

The gradient w.r.t
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\left[\int\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\left[\int q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right)d\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\right].

The second term is
\mathbf{0}
(same as the one in the proof of Theorem
, which is the expectation of a score function).
Since
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})=[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})]q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})
, the first term becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\left[\int\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\left[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})}\big[\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})\right\}\big]\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})}\bigg[\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\big[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big]-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\bigg],

where the last equality comes from that the second term above is
\mathbf{0}
.
This concludes the result.
∎

Therefore, if we can compute
\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\big[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big]
analytically, the MC gradient w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\bigg[\left\{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}^{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2})}\big[\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}^{s},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big]-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}^{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})\right\}\bigg],

where
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}^{s}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1})
such that the MC gradient
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
has a smaller variance than the MC gradient
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
).

Variance Reduction for MCVI Gradients with Control Variate

When deriving the variance reduction in Rao-Blackwellization in Equation (
), we use a variate
g_{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})
to decompose the variance (in the expectation form) so as to reduce the variance.
The method of
control variate
extends this idea by using a general variate
h(\boldsymbol{\omega})
.
Once again, we consider the parameter
\boldsymbol{\omega}
with its variational distribution
q(\boldsymbol{\omega})
, and we would like to estimate the expectation for any function
g(\boldsymbol{\omega})
:
\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})]
.
We have

\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})]=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\big[\underbrace{g(\boldsymbol{\omega})-h(\boldsymbol{\omega})+\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})]}_{G(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\big],

(2.33)

i.e.,
G(\boldsymbol{\omega})
is an unbiased estimator of
g(\boldsymbol{\omega})
.
Suppose
\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})]<\infty
, then the variance of
G(\boldsymbol{\omega})
is

\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[G(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]=\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]+\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]-2\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}),h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right].

Therefore, upon a careful choice of
h(\boldsymbol{\omega})
such that
\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]-2\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}),h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]<0
will reduce the variance of
\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[G(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]<\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]
.

Scaling control variate.

Having more control on the magnitude of variance reduction, we may define a scaled variance
\mu
for (
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\big[\underbrace{g(\boldsymbol{\omega})-\mu\left(h(\boldsymbol{\omega})-\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})]\right)}_{G(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\big];

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[G(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]+\mu^{2}\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]-2\mu\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}),h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right].

The optimal value for
\mu
is simply
\mu^{*}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left[g(\boldsymbol{\omega}),h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]/\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[h(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]
in this sense by optimizing the quadratic function of
\mu
.

Control variate with score function.

In the context of VI, we again consider the ELBO
{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in (
) and notations therein. Since the expectation of the score function
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
under
q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is zero:
\mathbb{E}_{q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})}[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda})]=\mathbf{0}
(see the proof of Theorem
).
Element-wise, we consider the
i
-th component
\lambda_{i}
of the variational parameter
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(see Equation (
)), and define the following functions

\displaystyle g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})

\displaystyle\equiv\{\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\lambda_{i})\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\lambda_{i})\right\};

\displaystyle h_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})

\displaystyle\equiv\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\lambda_{i}).

Therefore, the optical scale
\mu_{i}^{*}
for
i
-th component is
\mu_{i}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}\left[g_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}),h_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]/\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}_{q_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})}\left[h_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right]
.
This provides us with the
i
-th MC gradient by

\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}{\mathcal{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\approx\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\{\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\lambda_{i})\}\left\{\ln p({\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ln q(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\mid\lambda_{i})-\mu_{i}^{*}\right\},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}\sim q(\boldsymbol{\omega}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}).

The update follows again from the ascent method with this MC gradient.

(a)

(Plain) VI.

(b)

Amortized VI.

Figure 2.9:
Graphical model representation of latent variable models with variational inference. Green circles denote prior variables, orange circles represent observed and latent variables, and plates represent repeated variables.

2.5.7
Amortized Variational Inference

We considered the constrained framework for the variation Bayesian inference problem in Remark
and its graphical representation in Figure
.
The corresponding optimization over the ELBO is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{N},q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln p({\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{X}},\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})d\boldsymbol{\theta}d{\mathcal{Z}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\ln q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})d\bm{z}_{i}-\int q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\ln q_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}.

The limitation of the this optimization becomes evident with a large number of observations,
N
, as it results in significant computational inefficiency due to the lack of a memory mechanism to trace and utilize inferences from prior data points.

This problem is addressed through an “
amortization
” strategy. Rather than performing optimization for each data point individually, the computational cost is distributed over multiple instances, thereby alleviating the overall computational load
(
Kingma and Max 2013
;
Rezende et al. 2014
;
Rezende and Mohamed 2015
;
Ganguly et al. 2023
)
. To achieve this,
amortized VI
employs a stochastic function that transforms the observed variables into latent variables associated with the variational posterior distribution. The parameters of this distribution are learned as part of the optimization process; for example, the distribution can be modeled using neural networks.
Therefore, rather than maintaining distinct parameters
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}
for each hidden variable
\bm{z}_{i}
, the estimated function can predict latent variables for new data points without the need to re-optimize from scratch. This approach enhances computational efficiency and enables flexible reuse of pertinent information from past inferences on new, unseen data.
The caricature of the graphical representation for the contrained framework problem with amortized VI is depicted in Figure
.

Of course, it also comes with drawbacks: since the “global structure” is usually unknown to the user, a poorly designed amortization scheme can result in distributions with limited representational capacity. While one might propose using neural networks to achieve highly flexible
q(\bm{z})
distributions, the calculation of the (Monte Carlo approximation of the) variational lower bound necessitates that
\ln q(\bm{z})
remains tractable, which imposes another restrictive constraint.

Figure 2.10:
Graphical representation for VAE. Use the variational distribution
q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x})
to approximate the intractable posterior
p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x})
.

Example 45

(Variational Auto-Encoder (VAE))
.

The motivating for the amortized VI is so-called the variational auto-encoder (VAE)
(
Kingma and Max 2013
;
Rezende et al. 2014
)
. See Figure
.
We consider the following generative process:

\bm{z}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{z}\mid\mathbf{0},\bm{I}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\bm{x}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{z}_{i}),\mathrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{z}_{i}))),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}
are defined by deep neural network transforms of
\bm{z}
.
Our goal is to find the most likely configuration of the neural network parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
by maximum likelihood:

\mathop{\max}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ln p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{i}).

With plain VI, this is achieved by constrained EM algorithm (Section
) with maximizing over the ELBO iteratively:

\mathop{\max}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}s}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})}\left[\ln\frac{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})}\right].

The VAE, however, assumes a data-dependent posterior approximation
q(\bm{z}\mid\bm{x},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
with variational parameter
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
:

q(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i}),\mathrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{2}(\bm{x}_{i}))),

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{2}
can be transformations parameterized by neural networks.
Therefore, the corresponding maximization over ELBO is

\mathop{\max}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\left[\ln\frac{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{x}_{i})}{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\right].

With the reparameterization trick introduced in §
, we have

\bm{z}_{i}\sim q(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\implies\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{I}),\bm{z}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})\circledast\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i},

where
\circledast
represents the Hadamard product. And the corresponding ELBO becomes

\mathop{\max}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{p(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\left[\ln\frac{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\big(\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})\circledast\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\right\},\bm{x}_{i}\big)}{q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\big(\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})\circledast\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\right\}\mid\bm{x}_{i}\big)}\right].

The MC sample for the ELBO reduces to

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\left[\ln\frac{p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\big(\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})\circledast\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}^{s}\right\},\bm{x}_{i}\big)}{q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\big(\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\bm{x}_{i})\circledast\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}^{s}\right\}\mid\bm{x}_{i}\big)}\right],\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}^{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{I}).

Therefore, the parameters
\{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}\}
can be updated using stochastic MC gradients (backpropagation needs to be used to update the weights in neural networks).

æ
Chapter 2 Problems
6

1.

Suppose both
p(x)
and
q(x)
are probability density functions with

p(x)\propto q(x).

Show that
p(x)=q(x)
for all
x
.

2.

When we discuss Bayesian matrix decomposition methods, each model will be represented by a graphical representation, e.g., the GGG model in Figure
. Read Section
, draw the graphical representations for Bayesian linear models with zero-mean prior, semi-conjugate prior, and full conjugate prior, respectively.
Discuss the Markov blanket for each node.

3.

When setting
q^{(t+1)}_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})=p(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bm{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}),\,\forall\,i
in each iteration
t
of the E-step in the EM algorithm (Section
), show that the ELBO is tight such that
{\mathcal{F}}(\{q_{\bm{z}_{i}}(\bm{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})={\mathcal{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\right)
.

4.

When fitting distribution
q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to
p
by minimizing
D_{\mathrm{KL}}[q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\|p]
w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, it’s called
reverse/exclusive KL
. Show that it has the property of
mode-seeking
or
zero-forcing
, which means minimization forces
q
to be zero where
p
is zero and hence makes it concentrate on one of the modes.
On the other hand, when fitting
q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
to
p
by minimizing the KL divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}[p\|q_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]
w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, it’s called
forward/inclusive KL
. show that it has the property of
mass-covering
or
mean-seeking
, which means we must make sure that there is some mass under
q
wherever there is some mass under
p
.

Chapter 3
Regular Probability Models and Conjugacy

3.1
Conjugate Priors

I
In Section
, we briefly discussed conjugate priors.
Conjugate priors play a crucial role in Bayesian statistics, providing a convenient mathematical property that simplifies the computation of posterior distributions.
We now present the formal definition as follows.

Definition 46

(Conjugate Prior)
.

Given a family
\{p({\mathcal{X}}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}):\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}\}
of generating distributions, a collection of priors
p_{\omega}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
indexed by
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
is called a conjugate prior family if for any
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and any data, the resulting posterior equals to
p_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid{\mathcal{X}})
for some
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
.

In Bayesian inference, a prior distribution represents our beliefs about the parameters of a statistical model before observing any data. A prior is considered conjugate to a likelihood function if their combination results in a posterior distribution that belongs to the same family of distributions as the prior. This property facilitates analytical solutions, making the computation of the posterior more tractable.
A toy example of the Beta-Bernoulli model can enhance our comprehension of conjugate priors.

Example 47

(Beta-Bernoulli)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{N}\}
are drawn independently and identically distributed (i.i.d.) from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
\theta
, i.e.,
Bernoulli(x\mid\theta)
.

Beta(\theta\mid a,b)
distribution, with
a,b>0
, is conjugate to
Bernoulli(x\mid\theta)
, since the posterior density is
p(\theta\mid{\mathcal{X}})=Beta(\theta\mid a+\sum x_{i},b+N-\sum x_{i})
.

Conjugate priors make it possible to do Bayesian reasoning in a computationally efficient manner, as well as having the philosophically satisfying interpretation of representing real or imaginary prior data. Generally, there are basically two reasons why models with conjugate priors are popular
(
Robert et al. 2007
;
Bernardo and Smith 2009
;
Hoff 2009
;
Gelman et al. 2013
)
:

They usually allow us to derive a closed-form expression for the posterior distribution.

They are easy to interpret, as we can easily observe how the parameters of the prior change after the Bayesian updates.

3.2
Random Variables and Distribution

A random variable is a variable that assumes different values randomly that models uncertain outcomes or events. We denote the random variable itself with a lowercase letter in
normal fonts
, and its possible values with lowercase letters in
italic fonts
.
For instance,
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are possible values of the random variable
y
.
In the case of vector-valued variables, we represent the random variable as
{\mathbf{y}}
and one of its realizations as
\bm{y}
.
Similarly, we denote the random variable as
{\mathbf{Y}}
and one of its values as
\bm{Y}
when we are working with matrix-valued variables.
However, a random variable merely describes possible states and needs to be accompanied by a probability distribution specifying the likelihood of each state.

Random variables can be either discrete or continuous. A discrete random variable has a finite or countably infinite number of states. These states need not be integers; they can also be named states without numerical values. Conversely, a continuous random variable is associated with real values.

A probability distribution describes the likelihood of a random variable or set of random variables.
The characterization of probability distributions varies depending on whether the variables are discrete or continuous.

For discrete variables, we employ a
probability mass function (p.m.f., PMF)
. Probability mass functions are denoted with a capital
P
.
The probability mass function maps a state of a random variable to
the probability of that random variable taking on that state. The notation
P({\textnormal{y}}=y)
represents the probability that
y
equals
y
, with a probability of 1 indicating certainty and a probability of 0 indicating impossibility.
Alternatively, we define a variable first and use the
\sim
notation to specify its distribution later:
{\textnormal{y}}\sim P({\textnormal{y}})
.

Probability mass functions can operate on multiple variables simultaneously, constituting a
joint probability distribution
.
For example,
P({\textnormal{x}}=x,{\textnormal{y}}=y)
denotes the probability of
x=x
and
y=y
simultaneously, and we may also use the shorthand
P(x,y)
. Moreover, if the PMF depends on some known parameters
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, then it can be denoted by
P(x,y\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for brevity.

When dealing with continuous random variables, we represent probability distributions using a
probability density function (p.d.f., PDF)
instead of a probability mass function. For a function
p
to qualify as a probability density function, it must adhere to the following properties:

The domain of
p
must be the set of all possible states of
y
;

We do not require
p(y)\leq 1
as that in the PMF. However, it must satisfies that
\forall\,y\in{\textnormal{y}},p(y)\geq 0
.

Integrates to 1:
\int p(y)dy=1
.

A probability density function
p(y)
does not provide the probability of a specific state directly.
Instead, the probability of falling within an infinitesimal region with volume
\delta y
is given by
p(y)\delta y
.
Moreover, if the probability density function depends on some known parameters
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, it can be denoted by
p(x\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})
or
f(x;\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for brevity.

In many cases, our focus lies in determining the probability of an event, given the occurrence of another event. This is referred to as a
conditional probability
.
The conditional probability that
{\textnormal{x}}=x
given
{\textnormal{y}}=y
is denoted by
P({\textnormal{x}}=x\mid{\textnormal{y}}=y)
.
This can be calculated using the formula

P({\textnormal{x}}=x\mid{\textnormal{y}}=y)=\frac{P({\textnormal{x}}=x,{\textnormal{y}}=y)}{P({\textnormal{y}}=y)}.

This formula serves as the cornerstone in Bayes’ theorem (see Equation (
)).

On the contrary, there are instances when the probability distribution across a set of variables is known, and the interest lies in determining the probability distribution over a specific subset of them.
The probability distribution over the subset is referred to as the
marginal probability distribution
.
For example, suppose we have discrete random variables
x
and
y
, and we know
P({\textnormal{x}},{\textnormal{y}})
. We can find
P({\textnormal{x}})
using summation:

P({\textnormal{x}}=x)=\sum_{y}P({\textnormal{x}}=x,{\textnormal{y}}=y).

Similarly, for continuous variables, integration is employed instead of summation:

p(x)=\int p(x,y)dy.

Gamma, p.

, p.

Inverse-Gamma, p.

Truncated-Normal, p.

Inverse-Gaussian, p.

Chi-Square, p.

Inverse-Chi-Squared, p.

Nor-Inv-Chi-Squared, p.

Skew-Laplace, p.

Rectified-Normal, p.

Dirichlet, p.

Poisson, p.

Exponential, p.

Multi Gaussian, p.

Multi Student’s
t
, p.

Wishart, p.

Inverse-Wishart, p.

Normal-Inv-Wishart, p.

Table 3.1:
Links for common distributions.

In most of our Bayesian matrix decomposition developments, we express the models with univariate distributions.
While in the Gaussian model, we also apply multivariate distributions.
In this section, we provide rigorous definitions for common univariate distributions and their conjugate priors.
Table
provides an overview of the topics covered in this section.
Additionally, with special considerations, we also discuss the multivariate Gaussian distribution and its conjugacy in the next section.

Definition 48

(Gaussian or Normal Distribution)
.

A random variable
x
is said to follow the Gaussian distribution (a.k.a., a normal distribution) with mean and variance parameters
\mu
and
\sigma^{2}>0
, denoted by
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
, if

f(x;\mu,\sigma^{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-\mu)^{2}\right\}=\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{2\pi}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\tau}{2}(x-\mu)^{2}\right\}.

The mean and variance of
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
are given by

\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\mu,\qquad\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\sigma^{2}=\tau^{-1},

where
\tau
is also known as the precision of the Gaussian distribution.
Figure
illustrates the impact of different parameters
\mu,\sigma^{2}
for the Gaussian distribution.

Figure 3.1:
Gaussian probability density functions for different values of the mean and variance parameters
\mu
and
\sigma^{2}
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{N}\}
are drawn i.i.d. from a Gaussian distribution of
\mathcal{N}(x\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})
.
For conjugate Bayesian analysis, we may rewrite the Gaussian probability density function as follows:

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X}\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\prod^{N}_{i=1}\mathcal{N}(x_{i}\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})

(3.1)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-N/2}(\sigma^{2})^{-N/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+N\sum_{n=1}^{N}(x_{n}-\overline{x})^{2}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-N/2}(\sigma^{2})^{-N/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+NS_{\overline{x}}\right]\right\},

where
S_{\overline{x}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}(x_{n}-\overline{x})^{2}
and
\overline{x}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}
.
This form can help find the conditional posterior of Gaussian likelihood under
normal-inverse-Gamma
prior (Equation (
)).

Given fixed mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
parameters, we have

\displaystyle p(x\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(x\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})\propto\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}x^{2}+\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{2}}x\right\},

(3.2)

where “
\propto
” means “proportional to.”
Therefore, if we find a form conforming to the above equation, we can say the random variable
x
follows the Gaussian distribution
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
.
Refer to the example of a Bayesian
GGG
matrix decomposition model in Equation (
).

While the product of two Gaussian variables remains an open problem, the sum of Gaussian variables follows from a new Gaussian distribution.

Remark 49

(Sum of Gaussians)
.

Let
x
and
y
be two Gaussian distributed variables with means
\mu_{x},\mu_{y}
and variance
\sigma_{x}^{2},\sigma_{y}^{2}
, respectively.

When there is no correlation between the two variables, then it follows that

{\textnormal{x}}+{\textnormal{y}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{x}+\mu_{y},\sigma_{x}^{2}+\sigma_{y}^{2}).

When there exists a correlation of
\rho
between the two variables, then it follows that

{\textnormal{x}}+{\textnormal{y}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{x}+\mu_{y},\sigma_{x}^{2}+\sigma_{y}^{2}+2\rho\sigma_{x}\sigma_{y}).

Conjugate prior for mean of a Gaussian distribution and Normal-Normal model.

The Gaussian distribution serves as a conjugate prior for the mean parameter of a Gaussian distribution when the variance is fixed. To see this, suppose
{\mathcal{X}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}\}
are i.i.d. normal with mean
\theta
and precision
\lambda
, i.e., the likelihood is
\mathcal{N}(x_{i}\mid\theta,\lambda^{-1})
, where the variance
\sigma^{2}=\lambda^{-1}
is fixed, and
\theta
is given a
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{0},\lambda^{-1}_{0})
prior:
\theta\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{0},\lambda^{-1})
. Using Bayes’ theorem, “posterior
\propto
likelihood
\times
prior,” the posterior density is

p(\theta\mid{\mathcal{X}})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{N}(x_{i}\mid\theta,\lambda^{-1})\times\mathcal{N}(\theta\mid\mu_{0},\lambda_{0}^{-1})\propto\mathcal{N}(\theta\mid\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}),

where, given
\overline{x}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}+\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}}{\lambda_{0}+N\lambda}=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{\lambda_{0}+N\lambda}\mu_{0}+\frac{N\lambda}{\lambda_{0}+N\lambda}\overline{x},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+N\lambda.

Thus, the posterior mean is a weighted mean of the prior mean
\mu_{0}
and the sample average
\overline{x}
; the posterior precision is the sum of the prior precision and sample precision
N\lambda
.
We show that the Gaussian distribution is, itself, a conjugate prior for the mean parameter of a Gaussian distribution when fixing variance. This is often referred to as the
Normal-Normal model
, and this model can be used to provide evidence for the bimodal property of human heights
(
Schilling et al. 2002
)
.

Definition 50

(Student’s
t
Distribution)
.

A random variable
x
is said to follow the Student’s
t
distribution with parameters
\mu
,
\sigma^{2}>0
, and
\nu
, denoted by
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\tau(\mu,\sigma^{2},\nu)
, if

\displaystyle f(x;\mu,\sigma^{2},\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu+1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu}{2})}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{\nu\pi}}\times\left[1+\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{\nu\sigma^{2}}\right]^{-(\frac{\nu+1}{2})},

(3.4)

where
\sigma^{2}
is called the
scale parameter
, and
\nu
is the
degree of freedom
.
The distribution has fatter tails than a Gaussian distribution. The smaller
\nu
is, the fatter the tail.
As
\nu\rightarrow\infty
, the distribution converges towards a Gaussian.
A particular case of the Student’s
t
distribution is the
Cauchy distribution
,

{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{C}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
if
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\tau(\mu,\sigma^{2},1)
, i.e.,
\nu=1
.
The mean and variance of
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\tau(\mu,\sigma^{2},\nu)
are given by

\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mu,\,&\mathrm{if\,}\nu>1;\\
&\text{undefined},\,&\mathrm{if\,}\nu\leq 1.\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\nu}{\nu-2}\sigma^{2},\,&\mathrm{if\,}\nu>2;\\
&\infty,\,&\mathrm{if\,}1<\nu\leq 2.\end{aligned}\right.

Figure
compares different parameters
\mu,\sigma^{2},\nu
for the Student’s
t
distribution.

In Figure
, we vary the
\nu
parameter for the Student’s
t
distribution.
As
\nu
decreases, the distribution becomes more spread out, leading to fatter tails compared to a Gaussian distribution. This allows for more flexibility in modeling data with greater uncertainty or
outliers
since the Student’s
t
distribution has a greater probability of observing extreme values. In Bayesian modeling, the Student’s
t
distribution is often used as a prior for the mean parameter of a Gaussian likelihood, allowing for estimation of both the mean and precision of the data. This results in a
Student’s
t
-Normal
model.

(a)

Student’s
t
distribution by varying parameter
\nu
. When
\nu=100
, the distribution is very close to a Gaussian distribution.

(b)

Student’s
t
distribution by varying parameter
\sigma^{2}
.

Figure 3.2:
Student’s
t
distribution for different values of the parameters
\nu
and
\sigma^{2}
.

Definition 51

(Gamma Distribution)
.

A random variable
x
is said to follow the Gamma distribution with shape parameter
r>0
and rate parameter
\lambda>0
, denoted by
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{G}(r,\lambda)
, if

f(x;r,\lambda)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\lambda^{r}}{\Gamma(r)}x^{r-1}\exp(-\lambda x),&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}x\geq 0;\\
&0,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}x<0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{t-1}\exp(-x)dt
is the Gamma function, and we can just take it as a function to normalize the distribution into sum to 1. In special cases when
y
is a positive integer,
\Gamma(y)=(y-1)!
.
The mean and variance of
{\textnormal{x}}\sim\mathcal{G}(r,\lambda)
are given by

\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\frac{r}{\lambda},\qquad\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{x}}]=\frac{r}{\lambda^{2}}.

Specially, let
{\textnormal{x}}_{1},{\textnormal{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{x}}_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables drawn from
\mathcal{G}(r_{i},\lambda)
for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Then
{\textnormal{y}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\textnormal{x}}_{i}
