” means the performance is extremely poor.

Quantitative Strategy

After executing the GBT and IID algorithms for computing the interpolative decomposition of each asset’s alpha matrix, the state vector
\bm{r}
for each asset is saved, and the ten alphas with the largest mean selection during the 1,000 iterations are chosen (with a burn-in of 100 iterations, and a thinning of 5 iterations).

Then we follow the quantitative strategy in Algorithm
(in which case,
h=1
,
N=214
alphas,
M=10
alphas,
D=720
trading days, and
D_{\text{in}}=480
trading days).
The procedure shown in Algorithm
is a very simple quantitative strategy. However, the algorithm can show precisely how the IID method can work in practice.

The strategy using the alphas selected by the IID method is only slightly worse than the one selecting the
highest RankIC
alphas for the in-sample (IS) performance in terms of Sharpe ratio, annual return, and maximum drawdown; however, the IID performs better in the out-of-sample (OS) scenario, and this is what we actually want (see Table
and Figure
).
To evaluate the strategy, we also adopt the Randomized algorithm to compute the ID for comparison
(
Liberty et al. 2007
)
, termed
Randomized ID
. The Randomized ID performs even worse than BID with GBT (see Table
).
Though the IID does not select alphas with the highest RankIC values, this does not mean that the alpha selection procedure is meaningless for the following reasons:

1.

Pool size
: We only use a small alpha pool that only contains 214 alpha factors. When the number of alphas is approaching millions or even billions, the alpha selection procedure is expected to work better.

2.

Correlation
: The mean correlation of selected alphas across the ten assets of the IID method is smaller than the highest RankIC method. In this sense, the alphas of the latter method have high correlations and a low diversity. If the correlated alphas have low liquidity or perform poorly during a given period, the strategy’s risk might increase.

3.

Machine learning models
: In our test, we only use OLS to find the weight of each alpha For more complex models, e.g., neural networks or XGBoost models, the correlated alphas can cause multi-linear problems so that the performance and interpretability are hampered.

4.

Diversification
: Even if selecting the alphas with the highest RankIC can work well in practice, we also want to diversify the strategies so that we are not exposed to specific risks.
The IID method can help find different strategies.

æ
Chapter 10 Problems
6

1.

Determine the column ID for the matrix

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1&3&2\\
3&7&6\\
4&5&8\end{bmatrix}.

2.

Identify an example that demonstrates the effectiveness of the post-processing method described in Section
in reducing reconstruction errors.

3.

Prove the rank decomposition:
Any rank-
R
matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{M\times N}
admits the factorization

\underset{M\times N}{\bm{A}}=\underset{M\times R}{\bm{D}}\,\,\,\underset{R\times N}{\bm{F}},

where
\bm{D}\in\real^{M\times R}
has rank
R
, and
\bm{F}\in\real^{R\times N}
also has rank
R
, i.e.,
\bm{D}
and
\bm{F}
have full rank
R
.
The storage for the decomposition is then reduced or potentially increased from
MN
floating-point numbers to
R(M+N)
floating-point numbers.
Hint: use elementary transformation.

Part IV
Appendix

\nopagecolor

Chapter A
Appendix

A.1
Taylor’s Expansion

Theorem 95

(Taylor’s Expansion with Lagrange Remainder)
.

Let
f(x):\real\rightarrow\real
be
k
-times continuously differentiable on the closed interval
I
with endpoints
x
and
y
, for some
k\geq 0
. If
f^{(k+1)}
exists on the interval
I
, then there exists a
x^{\star}\in(x,y)
such that

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=f(y)+f^{\prime}(y)(x-y)+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(y)}{2!}(x-y)^{2}+\ldots+\frac{f^{(k)}(y)}{k!}(x-y)^{k}+\frac{f^{(k+1)}(x^{\star})}{(k+1)!}(x-y)^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{f^{(i)}(y)}{i!}(x-y)^{i}+\frac{f^{(k+1)}(x^{\star})}{(k+1)!}(x-y)^{k+1}.

The Taylor’s expansion can be extended to a function of vector
f(\bm{x}):\real^{n}\rightarrow\real
or a function of matrix
f(\bm{X}):\real^{m\times n}\rightarrow\real
.

The Taylor’s expansion, or also known as the
Taylor’s series
, approximates the function
f(x)
around the value of
y
by a polynomial in a single indeterminate
x
. To see where this series comes from, we recall from the elementary calculus course that the approximated function around
\theta=0
for
\cos(\theta)
is given by

\cos(\theta)\approx 1-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}.

That is, the
\cos(\theta)
is approximated by a polynomial with a degree of 2. Suppose we want to approximate
\cos(\theta)
by the more general polynomial with a degree of 2 by
f(\theta)=c_{1}+c_{2}\theta+c_{3}\theta^{2}
. An intuitive idea is to match the gradients around the
0
point. That is,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \cos(0)&=f(0);\\
\cos^{\prime}(0)&=f^{\prime}(0);\\
\cos^{\prime\prime}(0)&=f^{\prime\prime}(0);\\
\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\left\{\begin{aligned} 1&=c_{1};\\
-\sin(0)&=0=c_{2};\\
-\cos(0)&=-1=2c_{3}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

This makes
f(\theta)=c_{1}+c_{2}\theta+c_{3}\theta^{2}=1-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}
and agrees with our claim that
\cos(\theta)\approx 1-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}
around the 0 point. We shall not give the details of the proof.

A.2
Deriving the Dirichlet Distribution

We derive the Dirichlet distribution and its properties in this appendix.

A.2.1
Derivation

Let
{\textnormal{x}}_{1},{\textnormal{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{x}}_{K}
be i.i.d. random variables drawn from the Gamma distribution such that
{\textnormal{x}}_{k}\sim\mathcal{G}(\alpha_{k},1)
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. The joint p.d.f. of
{\textnormal{x}}_{1},{\textnormal{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{x}}_{K}
is given by

f_{{\textnormal{x}}_{1},{\textnormal{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{x}}_{K}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\prod_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}\exp(-x_{k}),&\text{ if all }x_{k}\geq 0.\\
&0,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

Define variables
{\textnormal{y}}_{k}
’s as follows:

\displaystyle{\textnormal{y}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\textnormal{x}}_{k}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\textnormal{x}}_{k}},\qquad\forall\,\,\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K-1\}

(A.1)

\displaystyle{\textnormal{y}}_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\textnormal{x}}_{K}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\textnormal{x}}_{k}}=1-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}{\textnormal{y}}_{k},

and variable
{\textnormal{z}}_{K}
as

{\textnormal{z}}_{K}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\textnormal{x}}_{k}.

(A.2)

Let random variables
\bm{X}=[{\textnormal{x}}_{1},{\textnormal{x}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{x}}_{K}]
,
{\mathbf{Y}}=[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{y}}_{K-1},{\textnormal{z}}_{K}]
, and their draws
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K}]
,
\bm{y}=[y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{K-1},z_{K}]
.
By
multidimensional transformation of variables
, we have

f_{{\mathbf{Y}}}(\bm{y})=f_{\bm{X}}(g^{-1}(\bm{y}))\left|\det\left[J_{g^{-1}}(\bm{y})\right]\right|,

where

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\vdots\\
x_{K-1}\\
x_{K}\end{bmatrix}=g^{-1}(\bm{y})=g^{-1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{K-1}\\
z_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\cdot z_{K}\\
y_{2}\cdot z_{K}\\
\vdots\\
y_{K-1}\cdot z_{K}\\
y_{K}\cdot z_{K}\end{bmatrix},

and the Jacobian matrix is given by

\displaystyle J_{g^{-1}}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{K-1}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{K}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{K-1}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{K}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}z_{K}&0&\cdots&0&y_{1}\\
0&z_{K}&\cdots&0&y_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&z_{K}&y_{K-1}\\
-z_{K}&-z_{k}&\cdots&-z_{K}&(1-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}y_{k})\end{bmatrix}=z_{K}^{K-1}.

This implies the joint p.d.f of
{\mathbf{Y}}
is

f_{{\mathbf{Y}}}(\bm{y})=f_{\bm{X}}(g^{-1}(\bm{y}))z_{K}^{K-1}=\frac{y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}\ldots y_{K-1}^{\alpha_{K-1}-1}y_{K}^{\alpha_{K}-1}}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\exp(-z_{K})z_{K}^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}-1}.

We realize that the right-hand side of the above equation is proportional to a p.d.f. of a Gamma distribution and

\int\exp(-z_{K})z_{K}^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}-1}dz_{K}=\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}).

Let
\alpha_{+}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}
,
this implies

f(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{K-1})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}.

We notice that
{\textnormal{y}}_{k}
’s are defined that
0<{\textnormal{y}}_{k}<1
for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, and
\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\textnormal{y}}_{k}=1
. This implies the above equation is the p.d.f. of the Dirichlet distribution. The construction shown above can be utilized to generate random variables from the Dirichlet distribution.

A.2.2
Properties of the Dirichlet Distribution

Suppose
{\mathbf{Y}}=[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{y}}_{K}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{K}]
, we here show the moments and properties of the Dirichlet distribution.

Mean of Dirichlet distribution.

Write out the expectation,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{1}]

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int y_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\bm{y}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha})dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int y_{1}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\int\cdots\int y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+1-1}\prod_{k=2}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+1)\prod_{k=2}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+1)}=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{+}},

where the last equality comes from the fact that
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
.

Variance of Dirichlet distribution.

Write out the variance
\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]^{2}
. Similarly, from the proof of the mean, we have

\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}^{2}]=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+2)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+2)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}=\frac{(\alpha_{i}+1)\alpha_{i}}{(\alpha_{+}+1)\alpha_{+}}.

This implies

\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]^{2}=\frac{(\alpha_{i}+1)\alpha_{i}}{(\alpha_{+}+1)\alpha_{+}}-(\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{+}})^{2}=\frac{\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i})}{\alpha_{+}^{2}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

Covariance of Dirichlet distribution.

Write out the covariance
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]
.
Again, similar to the proof of the mean, for
i\neq j
, we have

\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+2)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{j}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}=\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

This implies

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{i}]\mathbb{E}[{\textnormal{y}}_{j}]=\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}(\alpha_{+}+1)}-\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}^{2}}=\frac{-\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}^{2}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

Marginal distribution of
{\textnormal{y}}_{i}
.

By definitions in Equation (
) and Equation (
), we have

{\textnormal{z}}_{K}-{\textnormal{x}}_{i}\sim\mathcal{G}(\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i},1)
.
This implies

{\textnormal{y}}_{i}=\frac{{\textnormal{x}}_{i}}{{\textnormal{z}}_{K}}=\frac{{\textnormal{x}}_{i}}{{\textnormal{x}}_{i}+({\textnormal{z}}_{K}-{\textnormal{x}}_{i})}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i}).

which is from the fact about the p.d.f. of two independent Gamma random variables.

Aggregation property.

Suppose
[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2},\ldots,{\textnormal{y}}_{K}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{K}])
. Then, let
M={\textnormal{y}}_{i}+{\textnormal{y}}_{j}
, it follows that

\displaystyle[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},\ldots{\textnormal{y}}_{i-1},{\textnormal{y}}_{i+1},\ldots,{\textnormal{y}}_{j-1},{\textnormal{y}}_{j+1},\ldots,{\textnormal{y}}_{K},M]

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i+1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1},\alpha_{j+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K},\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}]).

Proof.

We realize that
M\sim\mathcal{G}(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j},1)
. Again by the multidimensional transformation of variables as shown at the beginning of this section, we conclude the result.
∎

The results can be extended to a more general case. If
\{A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{r}\}
is a partition of
\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, then

\left[\sum_{i\in A_{1}}{\textnormal{y}}_{i},\sum_{i\in A_{2}}{\textnormal{y}}_{i},\ldots,\sum_{i\in A_{r}}{\textnormal{y}}_{i}\right]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}\left(\left[\sum_{i\in A_{1}}\alpha_{i},\sum_{i\in A_{2}}\alpha_{i},\ldots,\sum_{i\in A_{r}}\alpha_{i}\right]\right).

Conditional distribution.

Let
{\textnormal{y}}_{0}=\sum_{k=3}^{K}{\textnormal{y}}_{i}
and
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}
, then
[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2},{\textnormal{y}}_{0}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}])
. Therefore, it follows that

f_{{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}(1-y_{1}-y_{2})^{\alpha_{0}-1}.

Similarly, we have

f_{{\textnormal{y}}_{2}}(y_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0})}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}(1-y_{2})^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}-1}=\mathrm{Beta}(y_{1}\mid\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}),

which is a p.d.f. of a Beta distribution. Therefore, the conditional p.d.f. of
{\textnormal{y}}_{1}\mid{\textnormal{y}}_{2}=y_{2}
is given by

f_{{\textnormal{y}}_{1}\mid{\textnormal{y}}_{2}=y_{2}}(y_{1}\mid y_{2})=\frac{f_{{\textnormal{y}}_{1},{\textnormal{y}}_{2}}(y_{1},y_{2})}{f_{{\textnormal{y}}_{2}}(y_{2})}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{1-y_{2}}\right)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\left(1-\frac{y_{1}}{1-y_{2}}\right)^{\alpha_{0}-1}\frac{1}{1-y_{2}},

which implies

\frac{1}{1-y_{2}}{\textnormal{y}}_{1}\mid{\textnormal{y}}_{2}=y_{2}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}).

Apply this procedure, we will have

\bm{Y}_{-i}\mid{\textnormal{y}}_{i}\sim(1-y_{i})\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{-i}),

where
\bm{Y}_{-i}
contains all the
K-1
variables except
{\textnormal{y}}_{i}
; and similarly for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{-i}
.

A.3
Existence of the Skeleton Decomposition

In linear algebra, the row rank and the column rank of a matrix are equal. In other words, we can also claim that the dimension of the column space and the dimension of the row space are equal
(
Lu 2021a
)
. This property is essential for proving the existence of the skeleton decomposition.
The proof is rather elementary.

of Theorem
.

The proof relies on the existence of such a nonsingular matrix
\bm{U}
, which is central to this decomposition method.

Existence of such nonsingular matrix
\bm{U}
.

Since matrix
\bm{A}
is of rank
R
, we can pick
R
columns from
\bm{A}
such that they are linearly independent.
Suppose we put the specific
R
linearly independent columns
\bm{a}_{i1},\bm{a}_{i2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{iR}
into the columns of an
M\times R
matrix
\bm{N}=[\bm{a}_{i1},\bm{a}_{i2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{iR}]\in\real^{M\times R}
.
The dimension of the column space of
\bm{N}
is
R
so that the dimension of the row space of
\bm{N}
is also
R
. Again, we can pick
R
linearly independent rows
\bm{n}_{j1}^{\top},\bm{n}_{j2}^{\top},\ldots,\bm{n}_{jR}^{\top}
from
\bm{N}
and put the specific
R
rows into rows of an
R\times R
matrix
\bm{U}=[\bm{n}_{j1}^{\top};\bm{n}_{j2}^{\top};\ldots;\bm{n}_{jR}^{\top}]\in\real^{R\times R}
.
The dimension of the column space of
\bm{U}
is also
R
, which means there are
R
linearly independent columns from
\bm{U}
. So
\bm{U}
is such a nonsingular matrix with size
R\times R
.

Main proof.

As long as we find the nonsingular
R\times R
matrix
\bm{U}
inside
\bm{A}
, we can find the existence of the skeleton decomposition as follows.

Suppose
\bm{U}=\bm{A}[I,J]
, where
I
and
J
are index vectors of size
R
. Since
\bm{U}
is a nonsingular matrix, the columns of
\bm{U}
are linearly independent. Thus the columns of matrix
\bm{C}
based on the columns of
\bm{U}
are also linearly independent (i.e., select the
R
columns of
\bm{A}
with the same entries of the matrix
\bm{U}
. Here
\bm{C}
is equal to the matrix
\bm{N}
we construct above, and
\bm{C}=\bm{A}[:,J]
).

As the rank of the matrix
\bm{A}
is
R
, if we take any other column
\bm{a}_{i}
of
\bm{A}
,
\bm{a}_{i}
can be represented as a linear combination of the columns of
\bm{C}
, i.e., there exists a vector
\bm{x}
such that
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.
Let
R
rows (entries) of
\bm{a}_{i}\in\real^{N}
corresponding to the row entries of
\bm{U}
be
\bm{r}_{i}\in\real^{R}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
(i.e.,
\bm{r}_{i}
contains
R
entries of
\bm{a}_{i}
). That is, select the
R
entries of
\bm{a}_{i}
’s corresponding to the entries of
\bm{U}
as follows:

\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]\in\real^{M\times N}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\bm{A}[I,:]=[\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{N}]\in\real^{R\times N}.

Since
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{x}
,
\bm{U}
is a submatrix inside
\bm{C}
, and
\bm{r}_{i}
is a subvector inside
\bm{a}_{i}
, we have
\bm{r}_{i}=\bm{U}\bm{x}
, which states that
\bm{x}=\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{r}_{i}
. Thus, for every
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, we have
\bm{a}_{i}=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{r}_{i}
. Combining the
n
columns of such
\bm{r}_{i}
into
\bm{R}=[\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2},\ldots,\bm{r}_{N}]
, we obtain

\bm{A}=[\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{N}]=\bm{C}\bm{U}^{-1}\bm{R},

from which the result follows.

In short, we first find
R
linearly independent columns of
\bm{A}
into
\bm{C}\in\real^{M\times R}
. From
\bm{C}
, we find an
R\times R
nonsingular submatrix
\bm{U}
. The
R
rows of
\bm{A}
corresponding to entries of
\bm{U}
can help to reconstruct the columns of
\bm{A}
.
∎
